| R11-B1 | Complete governed-tool Ask journey | DONE | Engineering | Both halves landed 2026-09-12. Parameter half: the clarification is a contract, not prose -- the refusal carries the required parameter names and the selected tool version, and Ask renders an input per parameter and re-asks pinning that version so the retry cannot answer from a different tool. Seeding half: the sample estate now publishes one parameterised governed tool (`accounts_by_branch`, a STRING parameter deliberately, because Ask sends strings and an INTEGER parameter would refuse the browser's own input) through the real maker-checker path -- draft, submit, then APPROVE by the script's separate checker identity, with nothing writing a PUBLISHED row directly. Proven by six tests: the question answers with `generation_source=GOVERNED_TOOL` and zero model routes configured; without the parameter it returns the structured 409 naming it; the author cannot approve their own tool; and an answer can be proposed as a tool and still needs the gate. Non-vacuity checked by mutation -- an unrelated question fails both journey tests. **Both remaining clauses resolved 2026-09-12, and the first was stale rather than open.** The propose-as-tool action *does* exist in the UI: `components/SaveAnalysisTool.tsx`, rendered from `AskScreen` whenever a run reaches COMPLETED, and it is carefully built -- it prepares the blueprint from the run's own stored SQL, requires the author to tick "I checked the SQL and parameter types; no source values are used as defaults" before anything is created, and says on success that the draft must be submitted for independent review. The row was written before it landed. What it did *not* have was a test, which for a surface whose whole job is to not put a tool into service is the wrong way round: 8 now cover it, and the attestation one fails when the `!reviewed` guard is removed, so the gate is measured rather than described. One real defect surfaced while testing it: the component called `postJson` directly for the blueprint and so bypassed `demoOr`, meaning the **first** button of the flow made a live network call against the demo estate and failed while the second button already had a fixture -- the flow could not be demonstrated at all and the failure read as a broken feature rather than as "not available here". The call now goes through `api/agents.fetchAnalysisToolBlueprint` with a fixture that repeats the demo answer's own statement (`AgentRunRead` does not carry the executed SQL, so the fixture cannot read it back and will not invent an unrelated one). **The live-stack clause is closed by measurement**: `scripts/verify_end_to_end.py` against the running deployment reports `Ask refuses a governed tool naming the input it needs -- 409 requires ['branch_code']` and then `Ask answers from the approved governed tool -- generation_source=GOVERNED_TOOL`, which is the refusal path the demo fixtures could not show. |
| R11-B2 | Measure live governed answer quality | DONE | Engineering | Unblocked 2026-09-12 by a real credential, and measuring it immediately found two things. An APPROVED `gemini-bank-sql` route now exists for `sample-bank`, created through the real maker-checker path by the new `scripts/seed_model_route.py` -- which drives `create_model_route` -> `submit_model_route` -> `decide_governance_review` with two identities rather than inserting an APPROVED row, and whose `--same-identity` mode is refused with 409 "maker-checker separation is required", so the control is demonstrated rather than assumed. The OpenAI key authenticates but its account returns `billing_not_active`, so Gemini is primary and OpenAI stays configured as the fallback. Generation posture is **ACTIVATABLE for the first time** -- and it read as inactivatable before partly because `quality_benchmark.py` constructed a bare `Settings()`, which reads the process environment and never `.env`, so the posture check answered from a different configuration source than the application. An embedding provider is configured too (`gemini-embedding-001`, 768 dimensions) and the persisted vector index has now been built for the first time -- 10 objects, signature recorded -- so the vector channel serves from `PERSISTED_INDEX` rather than always falling back to live embedding. **Two defects that only a live provider could expose:** the retrieval corpus contained no case that lexical matching could not solve, so it could not measure the signal it was fusing; five `semantic-*` cases were added whose wording shares no stem with the target, and all five miss, which is the honest recorded state of semantic retrieval here. And the persisted index covers `INDEXED_OWNER_TYPES` only, so a `GOVERNED_TOOL` candidate silently left the vector stage with no score while the live path scored it -- reported as `PERSISTED_INDEX / USABLE` with nothing saying a class had been dropped, and worth 6 points of recall-within-bound (0.7059 live vs 0.6471 indexed). Uncovered candidates are now embedded live alongside the index lookup, restoring parity at 0.7059 exactly, with `retrieval_vector_index_gap` reporting how many needed it. Baseline refreshed deliberately: hit@1 0.5882, recall-within-bound 0.7059, MRR 0.6373 over 17 cases -- lower than the old 12-case numbers because the corpus now contains cases the platform genuinely fails. **Execution-match scoring run against the live route 2026-09-12**, closing the clause that had never been run. `scripts/execution_match_benchmark.py` asks each question in `execution_match_corpus.json` through Ask on the running deployment, runs the case's gold SQL through the same governed query gateway, and compares **result sets, not SQL text** -- order-insensitive, alias-insensitive, numbers normalised, a multiset so a duplicated row is wrong. It stores no rows (verdicts and shapes only, ADR-0014), is never run by default because every case is a paid model call, and keeps a refusal (`NOT_ANSWERED`) separate from a wrong answer (`MISMATCH`) so the accuracy figure means something. Measured on Customer Master with the live Gemini route: **5 of 5 answered cases matched (100% execution accuracy), 2 not answered.** The matcher's rules are pinned by 9 tests that need no network. **The two unanswered cases are a real defect the measurement exposed, and its cause is identified, not guessed.** Both returned 409 `MISSING_TOOL_PARAMETERS: branch_code` from `accounts_by_branch`: *how many accounts of each account type* and *total current balance for each currency* were routed to a one-branch lookup that cannot answer either, and the user was asked for a branch code they never mentioned. The tool describes itself as returning accounts *with the account type, currency, status and current balance held there*, and those are exactly the questions' words: their lexical match scored **0.94 and 0.95** against a 0.55 threshold, while *how many accounts does each customer have* -- same subject, none of those terms -- scored 0.44 and was answered correctly by generation. Retrieval treats *mentions the tool's output columns* as *asks the tool's question* and ignores the tool's scope. **Not fixed here, deliberately**, because each remedy changes what Ask does in front of users and has a trade-off: rewriting this one description fixes only the demo; declining a tool whose required parameter the question never references generalises but is a heuristic that must keep *Which accounts are booked at a branch?* refusing correctly; down-weighting description matches in the lexical stage moves every ranking. **Decided and closed 2026-09-13** (the product owner delegated the call; commit `17ecd08`): the generalising remedy. The planner declines a tool when the caller did not supply a required parameter and the question does not mention it, records that as a REJECTED tool decision, and moves on to the next eligible tool or to generation. A tool chosen explicitly, a supplied input, a parameter named only by generic words (`id`, `code`) and a call with no question are unchanged, and *Which accounts are booked at a branch?* still refuses with 409 `branch_code`, re-checked live. **Re-run live: 7 of 7 answered cases matched, 0 not answered** (was 5 of 5 and 2 wrongly refused). 16 new test cases; removing the gate fails two. Semantic retrieval's design decision stays where it is recorded, on R11-S3 -- the vector channel re-ranks the authorized set and cannot discover, so configuring embeddings does not buy semantic search on its own -- and is not part of this row. Also closed the gap the live run exposed: **nothing scheduled the index rebuild.** `rebuild_vector_index` shipped with RT-1 reachable only from an operator endpoint whose UI does not exist, so an index went stale and the vector channel silently fell back to embedding every candidate on every query -- the exact cost the index exists to remove, presenting as correct behaviour. `run_vector_index_rebuild_pass` now runs from `run_scheduler_iteration` on R11-D11's cadence shape, with a no-op when no provider is configured (the shipped state) and the skip stamping the clock so an unconfigured deployment asks once per interval rather than every tick. 7 tests including an AST check that the scheduler still calls it. |
| R11-B3 | Prove workspace enforcement | DONE | Engineering | Landed 2026-09-12 with D9. A DENY suite in one real `ENFORCE` workspace, driven through `QueryExecutionGateway.execute` rather than `authorize` -- a proof that the policy engine denies while the query path never called it would be the exact defect that made this rollout hard to trust. Four cases: a principal carrying the allowed role but seated in no workspace is refused, because roles are identity-provider claims and not access; a member of one workspace is refused on another workspace's datasource, resolved from that datasource's own binding so the caller cannot pick the workspace that suits them; the same principal *naming* their own workspace while pointing at the other's datasource is still refused, which is the attack the previous case leaves open; and the member on their own workspace's source is allowed, without which any blanket denial would satisfy the other three. The falsifier is in the suite rather than argued: the same non-member's query succeeds in a SHADOW workspace and is recorded as a would-be denial, which is also what proves D9's divergence reporting end to end. Deployment posture is deliberately unchanged -- `workspace_authorization_posture` still defaults to OBSERVING and flipping it stays an operator decision; what changed is that the decision now has evidence. |

Evaluation now files into the existing `DataQualityIncident` sink under FRESHNESS_VIOLATION, fingerprinted per table so one row per table is structural: STALE opens (CRITICAL past 2x threshold), a still-stale table updates that same row rather than adding a second, recovery resolves it, and a relapse reopens the same row. The pass reads contracts of every status, not just ACTIVE -- `upsert_freshness_config` resets approval on each edit, so an ACTIVE-only query would strand an incident open with nothing left able to close it. Scheduled behind `freshness_evaluation_interval_minutes` (default 0) with `freshness_evaluation_max_tables` (500); default-off matters more here than for the agents, because an open CRITICAL incident fails governed tools closed via DQ-3. Remaining when this row first landed: **nothing wrote `FreshnessObservation`** outside tests -- reading a watermark column needs a connector capability that does not exist, and profiles are value-free per ADR-0014, so an approved contract with no observation evaluates STALE and says so. Observation production was part of this row's acceptance, which is why it did not close on scheduling and UI alone. Also no bulk evaluated-state route, so the panel reads per contract, bounded at 25. **Decided and closed 2026-09-13** (the product owner delegated the call). ADR-0014 gained an addendum, under its own revisit trigger, permitting exactly one statistic: `MAX(<watermark column>)` for an ACTIVE freshness contract, only on a column the catalog types as a date or time, read through the query gateway as the scheduler's identity, stored only as the observation's timestamp and pruned by the contract's `retention_days`. `freshness_observation.observe_freshness_for_datasource` produces the observations, and the scheduled pass now observes before it evaluates. The statement is a sqlglot expression quoted per dialect, never formatted text; a masked or tokenized read stores nothing; a refusal is counted by reason code and never worked around, so a policy denying STALE reads to everyone denies the observer too and the count shows it. The sweep's audit record carries counts and reason codes only. 12 tests; removing the temporal-column check, the ACTIVE filter or the masking check each fails its test. **Live on the sample estate:** a contract on `customer.account.opened_at` created by one steward and approved by another; the scheduled pass read the watermark through the gateway (execution COMPLETED, 1 row, `customer.account`), stored one observation and evaluated from `data_watermark` -- STALE and CRITICAL, correctly, because the sample data is more than two years old against the one-year maximum threshold. Editing the contract back to PENDING_APPROVAL let the next pass resolve the incident, leaving nothing open, and Ask on Customer Master still answered afterwards. The audit record read back as `{contracts_read: 1, observed: 1, outcomes: {OBSERVED: 1}}`. |

It is now granted to `workspace_owner`. The load-bearing test refuses `EXPORT` and allows `READ_METADATA` for the same caller, same roles, same workspace, which no arrangement of role strings can produce. A single `transport=` seam now lets an Object-Lock-enforcing httpx double run the real provider -- SigV4, canonical query string, lock headers, version pinning, XML parsing and the re-checksum all unmodified -- so a stubbed provider cannot be mistaken for proof. **F01 stays partial**: a real bucket's IAM, bucket policy and `s3:BypassGovernanceRetention` are not modelled, and hold-outlives-retention is unreachable in a verification window at the 2555-day default. The role ceiling is checked before any policy is loaded, so no ALLOW policy can widen it into `READ_DATA`: `tests/test_audit_export_authorization.py` has the same caller, in the same workspace, export the ledger and be refused `READ_DATA` with `ROLE_DOES_NOT_PERMIT_ACTION`. Migration `b7e3d1f9a254` seeds an organization-wide `Auditor` -> `auditor` access rule, by the principle the original seeded rules follow (each IdP role maps onto what it could already do): without it an enforcing workspace refused auditors the export their role is admitted to, and the rule grants nothing that reads data. Verified up and down on a scratch Postgres database. The row stays PARTIAL for F01 alone: the real-bucket evidence above. |

The genuine gap was visibility, and it was total: nothing outside `aida.delivery_intents` read the ledger, so a wedged worker and an all-night refusing destination were indistinguishable from an empty queue anywhere but the worker's own logs. `probe_delivery_backlog` extends the existing `/health/ready` surface rather than adding a parallel one, mirroring the outbox probe exactly: bounded, optional, never DOWN merely for being large, DOWN only when unreadable. Two decisions carry the value: age is anchored to `requested_at`, not `attempted_at`, so a day-old message retried every five minutes cannot read as fresh; and the reading includes `worker=enabled or disabled`, because `delivery_worker_enabled` defaults off and alerting that cannot tell a deliberately-idle deployment from a wedged one fires on every default install and gets muted. SIEM and notification intents share the ledger and are counted separately so they stay distinguishable. **F04 remains destination-unverified**: every automated test uses a loopback stub, which by construction exercises no vendor TLS, auth, rate limiting or payload validation. | R11-B11 | Protect the browser journey in CI | DONE | Engineering | Landed 2026-09-12. There was no browser harness at all -- the `ui-next` vitest suite is jsdom-only and loads no CSS, so nothing exercised the shipped artifact. A new CI job named ui-journey drives connect, scan, describe, review, Ask and evidence through the **real nginx image**, not the Vite dev server, because the two differ in exactly the ways that break a deployment: SPA fallback, base paths, which prefixes are proxied, header forwarding. It is the behavioural counterpart to the existing proxy-contract job, which proves the proxy *routes* with curl; this proves the application *works* across it. Reload, deep links and denied access are covered; 12 tests, `retries: 0`, three consecutive full runs plus five repeats of one test, and the one 29s cold-start outlier was fixed at the cause (the readiness check now waits on the proxy-to-upstream path, because `/health` is answered by nginx without touching the upstream) rather than absorbed into a timeout. Least-privilege seating is real and forced by a constraint worth recording: `VITE_DEV_ROLES` is baked in at build time, so six header-asserted identities would mean six images -- the suite instead uses the existing `VITE_AUTH_MODE=proxy`, where the browser asserts nothing and an authenticating proxy is the authority. Two journey steps were mis-located in the brief and corrected against the tree: "connect a source" is on Administration, not Sources, and "scan" is on Home. **The honest limit: the upstream is a contract-shaped stub**, with role tuples read from the generated surface-control matrix and gated against drift by `tests/test_journey_stub_contract.py`, so this covers the UI's behaviour under least privilege and *not* FastAPI's ABAC, which remains the Python suite's job. The full estate -- Postgres, Redis, Neo4j, Temporal, Redpanda, MinIO, plus a provider API key for Ask -- is not a non-flaky CI job. It also earned its keep immediately by surfacing five UI defects that only a least-privilege seat reveals, **all fixed the same day**: two paged screens swallowed a refused next page in a bare `catch {}` so the list silently stopped growing -- on an audit ledger that turns a truncated record into an apparently complete one; Ask had no 403 branch and told a refused caller "the question could not be answered", which reads as a broken platform; a refused organization list rendered as an empty dropdown, meaning "this tenant has no organizations"; and the scope bootstrap's single `Promise.all` let one 403 discard the two axes the caller *could* read, which is why this suite had to widen an Analyst identity with an extra role to get past it. The scope bootstrap now degrades per axis and keeps the server's own message. |

| R11-B18 | Notice when an approved model disappears upstream | DONE | Engineering | Landed 2026-09-12, the same day the failure it exists for happened here: a route approved for `gemini-2.0-flash`, which Google had retired, read as entirely healthy -- APPROVED, credentialed, selected -- while every generated answer failed with a 404 nothing connected back to the route. The two same-day fixes (a pre-flight model check when drafting, and 404 joining the statuses that reach the approved fallback) are **not detection**: registration-time validation says nothing about the months afterwards, and a fallback that rescues an answer still leaves a dead primary nobody has been told about. `run_model_route_reachability_pass` sweeps every APPROVED route on a cadence from `run_scheduler_iteration`, and three restraints are the design. **It lists, it never generates** -- listing models is free and generating is not, and a health check that cost money per route per sweep would be switched off, which is worse than absent because it still appears in the configuration. **It never touches `status`** -- an approval is a human decision taken through maker-checker, so reachability is recorded beside it in three nullable columns (migration `090b3be72b67`, downgrade exercised) and a sweep can never revoke what a person decided. **UNKNOWN is its own answer** and is never collapsed into UNREACHABLE, because a network failure, a rate limit or an unprobeable provider all mean "could not tell", and reporting those as retirements would train an operator to ignore the one real one. NULL means never checked, which is a third distinct fact the summary reports separately. Only a *change* of status is audited, since an audit trail that repeats itself every six hours is one nobody reads. Verified live against the real providers: the retired model came back UNREACHABLE with an actionable detail, the current route REACHABLE, three on-prem routes honestly UNKNOWN. That run also fixed a misreading before it shipped -- an OpenAI route whose account returns `billing_not_active` for every generation still **lists** its model perfectly well, so it reads REACHABLE; the module now says plainly that REACHABLE means the model exists and not that generation works, because a quota problem is visible the first time anyone asks a question while a retirement is not. Operator-visible through `/health/ready` as `model_routes.detail`, read from the recorded state so a readiness scrape never makes a provider call. 8 tests. |

| R11-X2 | Triage unused tables | DONE | Engineering | `slo_measurement` is resolved -- R11-D10 chose retire and migration `f3a91c27b5de` dropped it with `slo_definition`. Also dropped since this row was last written: `graph_perspective` and `policy_native_sync_request`. R11-C8's migration added no tables, only columns with real readers, so the reversal ledger needs no triage. **The row does not close, because re-running the analysis found two unused tables it never listed** -- and finding them required handling three shapes a naive grep misses: raw CREATE TABLE inside `op.execute` (how the SLO and ABAC tables were made), `op.drop_table` via a module constant, and a second model package under `src/atlas/modules/` holding 54 of the 189 tables, which is where both of these live and why the original sweep of `src/aida/` missed them. `business_assignment_rule`: nothing constructs or selects it, and while `business_graph.assign` accepts a rule id, no caller supplies one. **`isolation_boundary` is worse than unused and was fixed on the spot**: `workspace.isolation_boundary_id` carries a real foreign key with RESTRICT, the workspace-create API forwarded the value unvalidated, and nothing anywhere inserts an `IsolationBoundary` -- so every non-NULL value a client could send was a guaranteed IntegrityError shaped like a server fault. It now refuses with a 422 naming the field, tenant-scoped so another organization's boundary cannot be borrowed; 4 tests. Reachable and unusable is a worse state than unused. **`delivery_attempt` resolved 2026-09-12: reader, emphatically not retire.** Its own docstring says it exists so an outage is "legible after the fact" -- how many times, how far apart, with what error, whether the destination ever answered -- and the intent row carries only current state, so this is precisely the half you need when something has gone wrong and the current state says "FAILED". It was never truly write-only either: the notification runbook read it, by handing the operator a five-line `JOIN` to type into `psql`, which is a reader in the sense that a person with database credentials is a reader. `scripts/delivery_history.py` is the reader. **Deliberately a script, not an endpoint**, because R11-X5 measured ~178 routes with no UI and concluded the platform's problem is surfaces built ahead of their front doors: a 179th would have moved this from "no reader" to "no reachable reader", and the operator already runs `seed_model_route.py` and `verify_end_to_end.py` from the setup guide. Two readings the output makes explicit because both are misread: an intent with **no attempt rows was never tried** (almost always the worker being off) which is a different problem with a different fix from having been tried and refused; and a **Teams 2xx is not a receipt**, so `receipt_proven` is `None` there rather than `True` or `False` -- the 202 is a real answer from the Workflows trigger, so calling it a failure would be as wrong as calling it a receipt. Verified against real rows produced through the real path, not fixtures: 9 intents, 9 attempts, connection-refused against the discard port, and the destination rendered `http://127.0.0.1:9/#1b128a381050` -- the webhook path a bearer credential lives in never reaches the row, pinned from the reader's end by a test because that is where the value is about to be printed. **Producing those rows exposed a much larger defect, now fixed** (see the compose note below). 10 tests. **Decided and closed 2026-09-13 (the product owner delegated the call): all five retired** by migration `c4e7b2d9a613`, each for its own reason. The two ADR-0018 placeholders go with an addendum to that ADR rather than silently. `isolation_boundary` -- the STRICT hard wall -- had a table, a workspace foreign key and an API field, but nothing ever created a boundary and nothing enforced one, so a workspace carrying it would have *looked* protected; `business_assignment_rule` had a table and `business_assignment.rule_id`, but no rule was ever built or evaluated. The addendum keeps the intent and changes the order of work: a hard wall is built together with its enforcement, rules together with their evaluator, and until then an information barrier is a classification attribute plus a policy denying across it. The three write-only tables each had a better home already: `contract_sla_record` was inserted by every *read* of the SLA status endpoint -- a GET that wrote -- and the status is now computed from the violation ledger it was copied from; `studio_test_run` duplicated the `studio.change_set.test` audit record and the per-question `studio_eval_result` rows; and `procedure_tool_generation_record`'s provenance moves to a `governed_tool.version.generated_from_procedure` audit record. All five were empty on the development estate. The migration was run both ways on a scratch Postgres database -- the migration-ORM drift gate passes at head, a downgrade restores all five tables and both columns, a re-upgrade removes them -- and the development database is left for the stack's own `migrate` service on its next build, because the running images still map the old columns. **The API change is deliberate and versioned:** the workspace responses lose `isolation_boundary_id` and the studio test result loses `id`, `created_at` and `updated_at`; no screen read any of them, and a client still *sending* `isolation_boundary_id` gets a 422 naming it rather than silently losing a wall it asked for. the API version (`__version__` in `src/aida/__init__.py`) is 2.0.0 accordingly, the OpenAPI baseline and `ui-next` types are regenerated, and 4 new tests pin the retirement. **The compose defect:** `compose.yaml` had no `env_file` and each app service declared an explicit ~35-name `environment` list, so a setting an operator was told to put in `.env` reached the container only if it happened to be on that list. Five of the fifteen off-by-default flags were; `AIDA_DELIVERY_WORKER_ENABLED`, `AIDA_GOVERNANCE_NOTIFICATIONS_ENABLED`, the Slack and Teams webhook URLs, the reviewer-agent controls and **every task agent's interval** were not -- so those features were unconfigurable in Docker while the configuration looked correct, which is the worst possible pairing and is why this went unnoticed. All seven app services now read `.env` (`required: false`, so a fresh clone still starts), with the container-internal addresses still explicit so Compose precedence keeps a host-side `.env` from redirecting a container at localhost -- `tests/test_compose_environment.py` pins exactly that, because removing one from the block as a tidy-up would fail as though Postgres were down. |

Getting discovery means an additional stage that searches the index first and applies the policy filter to the *results*; output safety is unchanged, what changes is that the search touches vectors of objects the caller cannot see, which is a timing signal rather than a disclosure. Needs an owner and an ADR rather than a refactor, and should be additive so the safe ordering stays available per query. Reasoning and alternatives: `Docs/10-architecture/19-embeddings-design.md`. |

| R11-S9 | Reduce configuration | DONE | Architecture + Engineering | Un-deferred 2026-09-12 at the product owner's request ("are we over-engineered"), and the first clause -- the inventory -- is done and generated: `scripts/generate_configuration_inventory.py` writes `Docs/40-engineering/13-configuration-inventory.md` from the `Settings` class, and `tests/test_configuration_inventory.py` fails when it is stale. **The headline finding is the opposite of the one expected: there is no dead configuration.** All 242 settings are read by something; 51 ship switched off, empty or zero, which is a triage list, not waste. Getting to that answer took three rounds, and each round's wrong answer would have been actively harmful, because every miss lands in the "retire this" list -- the one list somebody acts on. A naive attribute walk reported 15 dead settings; twelve were the task agents' `getattr(settings, f"{key}_agent_interval_minutes")` and `reserved_principals`' `model_fields` walk filtering on a suffix constant, and the last three were exposed by properties on `Settings` itself (`max_query_estimate_cost` returns `max_postgres_plan_cost`), which excluding the config module to avoid counting validators had hidden. The generator now resolves f-strings, `endswith`/`startswith` over module constants, and one hop through a config member. A zero ratchet is now enforced, which is the row's own wording made mechanical: a setting that is neither enabled with evidence nor retired should not exist, and today none does. The generator decides nothing else -- enable-or-retire per feature is a judgement, and a script proposing retirements would be read as having made them. **Triaged and closed 2026-09-13 on the product owner's delegation.** All 51 settings that shipped off, empty or zero were decided one by one. 50 remain, each with a recorded decision in `scripts/configuration_decisions.py`, rendered as a Decision column in section 2 of the inventory; `test_every_setting_that_ships_off_carries_a_decision` fails when a setting ships off undecided or a decision outlives its setting. 30 are *Supplied* -- a value only the deployment has (an issuer, a vault, a key, a destination), where empty means not configured and there is nothing to enable; 3 are *Off by design* -- the Neo4j read path pending INV-9's drill, the reviewer agent (refused in production by R11-C3) and the kill switch; 16 are *Opt-in*, each naming its precondition -- a service to run, a contract to approve, a verdict change to review; and 1 is *Blocked* on R11-B9 (audit-archive legal hold). **One retirement:** `dq_itsm_webhook_enabled` gated its own URL a second time -- the switch on with no URL and a URL with the switch off both failed -- so setting the URL is now the opt-in. `Settings` forbids unknown keys, so a deployment still setting the old switch fails at startup and is told, which a test pins. **No default was flipped on:** each opt-in either needs something the platform cannot supply or changes a verdict an estate should review first, and turning one on to shrink the count is the move this row warns against. `lineage_cache_enabled` had no test at all, so calling it Opt-in would have rested on nothing; three tests now pin that off never touches the cache, a hit is served without building and a miss builds and stores. Removing the cache gate, or any one decision, fails its test. |
| R11-S10 | Consolidate navigation and review surfaces | DONE | Architecture + Engineering | Closed at the adopted journey-grouping/41-destination scope, not the original 19-screen target. Remaining scope-axis and review-surface consolidation is explicitly retained in R11-S13; do not repeat the completed navigation change. Un-deferred and landed 2026-09-12 at the product owner's request. The sidebar had been grouped into eight work areas since UX-20, but the **route table was 44 siblings** -- so the grouping existed only while the sidebar was visible, and a pasted link said nothing about its job. `SCREEN_JOURNEY` is now the single source and the shell's navigation reads its groups from it instead of declaring a second, unchecked copy. **44 destinations -> 41.** The three task-agent screens were 34-54 line wrappers over one console differing only by `kind` -- the component's own docstring already said they share one runtime server-side -- so they are one console with the agent as a filter. `parsed-lineage-review` folded into the review surface as a second named queue rather than a merged one: the backend *was* checked first, `GET /v1/governance/reviews/queue` is genuinely cross-type, but parsed lineage edges live in six parser tables with their own decision endpoint and bulk rules, so a fake union is one a bulk action would silently half-apply. Policy refusals moved Reviewer -> Auditor because nothing on that screen can be decided, and filing it beside the queues implied a third thing waiting for a reviewer; Reviewer went 4 -> 2. Every one of the 44 old routes is aliased and swept by test, rewritten with `replaceState` so an alias never costs a Back press. Two defects found on the way: `useUnsavedChanges` installed `beforeunload` but returned an in-app confirm **no caller ever invoked and the shell could not reach**, so every sidebar click silently discarded a half-written description; and `DescriptionDraftsScreen` was the last survivor of the hand-rolled `pushState` + synthetic `hashchange` pattern F09 removed. The shared scope layer was **confirmed already present and not reinvented** -- but its migration is half-finished (15 screens still self-fetch, and only the datasource axis was ever migrated; project, workspace and domain never were), which needs its own row rather than being bundled into an information-architecture change. Also named and left: relationship candidates are decided on two screens, which is genuinely one surface differing by scope filter, but the cross-source one also owns discovery scans and grant boundaries, so merging is a redesign. Gates: typecheck clean, 90 files / 783 tests, build clean, frontend reachability exit 0. |
| R11-S13 | Complete remaining scope and review-surface consolidation | PARTIAL | Frontend + Architecture | **Five of the review's six merges landed 2026-09-16; the sixth is declined with reasons, which is what the review asks for.** 41 destinations to 38, every retired route aliased and swept by test. **M6:** `OrgPicker.tsx` was a second organization picker the shell never mounted -- `ScopePicker` has been the live one throughout -- so D01's own instruction ("wire it into the shell nav or delete it, and remove this entry either way") took its second branch. Component, test and reachability allow-list entry deleted together, so the frontend allow-list is **3 documented exceptions to 2**. Its only real coverage was of `lib/org`'s provider rather than of the picker, so those assertions moved to a new `lib/org.test.tsx` instead of dying with it. The shared `snav__persona` CSS was kept: it is live in `PersonaNav`. **M5:** `RegisterDatasourceForm` is mounted in Sources as well as Administration -- the same component, one endpoint, no second POST path -- so registering a source and then testing its connection is one journey instead of two screens. Gated on `CONNECTION_ROLES` exported from `SourcesScreenAdmin` rather than re-listed, because the create-datasource and test-connection endpoints accept the same two roles: an operator offered step one must be offered step two. Administration keeps its copy -- lifting the fourth step out of a five-step onboarding wizard would leave a hole in the middle. **M3:** Documentation worklist, Description drafts and Data dictionaries are one workspace on `?view=priorities\|drafts\|imports`. Two snags were handled rather than smoothed: the tabs read **two different scope axes** (org via `useOrgId`, project via `useScopeSelection`) and the workspace states which is in front; and a tab switch is `patchQuery`, which **deliberately bypasses the shell's unsaved-changes choke point**, so the tab bar asks `lib/unsavedChanges` itself -- without that, switching away from a half-written `DescriptionEditor` draft would have discarded it in silence. **M4:** `OntologyManager` had exactly one live importer, a button in the Unified lineage header, so authoring the concepts and mappings that say what the business *means* was reachable only from a graph screen. Business Meaning is now an entry point too -- a dialog, not a fourth `?view=` tab, because that axis selects which read is in front and an unsaved JSON draft must not sit behind a tab switch. The graph keeps its contextual shortcut, as the review requires. **M1:** Lineage and Unified lineage were two sidebar items over one question, both scoped by `?ds=` and both selecting with `?node=`, so which one you opened decided which answer you got. One destination, three views (`explain\|graph\|impact`), and **every `?view=` spelling the route ever answered to still resolves** -- absent and `narrated` to Explain, `graph` to the merged graph, which is a superset of the bounded one that value used to open. **Two latent defects were fixed with it rather than inherited:** the unified-lineage route declared neither `depth` nor `direction` while the screen both read them and wrote all three of `node`/`depth`/`direction` from its graph-question form, so pasting the link that button produced normalised both away and the impact query silently reverted to depth 5 in both directions; and `navigateTo`/`App.navigate` tested ids with `isScreenId` ("is this live") rather than resolving them, so `EvidencePane`'s "Impact" cross-link -- which names the unified-lineage screen id through `CrossLinks`' deliberately untyped `screen: string` -- **would have landed on Overview**. Both doors now resolve through `RETIRED_SCREEN_ALIASES`, the same contract a pasted URL gets. **M2 declined, and the decline is the review being followed.** Its own constraint is "do not merge different permission or publication contracts", and the relationship/cross-source split is three contracts rather than a presentation difference: the scope axis is datasource read authorization versus ADR-0017 domain-grant authorization; the two read models are not the same shape (review-queue items carry `diff`, computed lineage-impact ordering and a per-row `can_review`, while the cross-source list is a bare `RelationshipCandidateRead` with none of them, so maker-checker is enforced per row on one side and as a server refusal rendered as prose on the other); and only the cross-source side owns the domain-scoped discovery writes with their 403-to-grant-request translation, while only the local side owns bulk decision. A merged bulk action over a mixed set would half-apply, which is exactly the failure R11-S10 refused for parsed lineage. This row already called it a redesign and nothing found here contradicts that. Evidence: **99 files / 899 frontend tests passed** (97/866 on this tree beforehand), `npm run typecheck` and `npm run build` clean, `scripts/check_frontend_reachability.py --check` exit 0 with 218 reachable modules and 2 allow-listed exceptions, `tests/test_frontend_reachability_gate.py` 10 passed, `scripts/check_proxy_contract.py` clean. One defect was found in the parent session's own R11-FP08 hunk while verifying this work and fixed here: `DescriptionActionSubject.subjectType` had been widened to include `ROUTINE` without widening `requestDescriptionWithdrawal`'s own parameter, which held `tsc` red until the API client's union was widened to match the route contract it already had. **Items 15/17, first stewardship workspace slice, landed 2026-09-19.** The action-to-destination map is `Docs/10-architecture/23-stewardship-action-map.md`, linked from design 21 section 17: every action on Stewardship, Playbooks, Task agents, Negative knowledge, Relationships and Cross-source with the API's `require_roles`, the UI gate, the deep link before and after and its section-17 home; stewardship routes that only ever existed on the server are recorded as never surfaced, not lost. `#/steward/stewardship` is now one workspace on `?view=`: Work queue (default -- the unowned backlog and ownership-expiry banner), Bulk actions (the four `bulk-*` filter actions) and Automation (`PlaybooksScreen`, unchanged). The old page's two panels were split with no change to any endpoint, request body, role or control enablement. `#/playbooks` is retired through `RETIRED_SCREEN_ALIASES` to `?view=automation`; a pre-workspace link carrying `action`/`field`/`pattern` still opens Bulk actions with its filter (`stewardshipViewFrom`), and the Work queue is written `view=queue` whenever that filter is present so it cannot reopen Bulk actions. Destinations 38 to 37; the palette still finds playbooks. The tab bar asks `lib/unsavedChanges`, and the bulk form now reports an edited, unrun action; tabs follow the ARIA keyboard model (roving tabindex, wrapping arrows, Home/End, a declined prompt keeps view and focus). Task agents, Documentation, Negative knowledge, Business Meaning and the Review queue stay destinations. The decision against a merged relationship/cross-source queue holds: Relationships links to Cross-source on the selected source's domain, and Cross-source links each domain source to its own Relationships queue. Verified in jsdom: 8 files / 162 tests re-run at integration (the agent's wider run: 18 files / 264, including the a11y sweep's three stewardship rows); ten mutations each failed their intended tests; `npm run build` clean; the frontend reachability gate exits 0. Browser acceptance not run. **Remaining:** browser acceptance of the three views, the legacy links and the keyboard (the 2026-09-16 merges' Playwright specs were not executed either); 17B -- Catalog row selection feeding Bulk actions through an existing write path (immediate `bulk-*` or reviewed `stewardship/bulk-operations`, a product decision) with any preview read-only, which also retires Catalog's disabled "Certify..." stub; unsaved-work reporting in the Playbooks create form; the negative-knowledge advanced Work queue filter; and a decision on the role gap in which DataSteward and MetadataReviewer can decide relationship and same-object candidates but cannot list them, which Cross-source currently shows as an empty queue rather than a refusal. **Prior scope, retained:** Explicit successor for S10's recorded remainder: migrate remaining self-fetching screens across datasource/project/workspace/domain scope; decide relationship/cross-source review consolidation without losing discovery or grant boundaries. Existing route aliases and completed 41-destination grouping stay. Reopen when the next agreed journey requires these changes; the old 19-screen target is not an automatic instruction. |

It surfaced 2 failures and investigating them found 3 more stale claims, all repaired against the tree: a promised query-gateway module under src/atlas that was never created, a claimed entrypoints package under src/atlas, `identity_tenancy` described as the lone 69-line scaffold (six modules, 9,649 lines exist), "1 of 21 modules exists", and two claims about module migrations and `pytest src/atlas` that R11-X4 had already made false. The one exemption kept is per-*name*, not per-package: `service.py`/`repository.py`/`router.py`/`contracts.py` each exist six times over, so an unqualified basename has no unique resolution; the full paths behind them are checked. A floor test fails loudly if the blind spot returns. |

| R11-C1 | Verify retained migrations and existing ontology journey | DONE | Platform lead | Rechecked read-only 2026-09-12: `alembic current` and `alembic heads` both report `090b3be72b67 (head)`; `to_regclass` confirms both `ontology_head` and `ontology_version` exist in the configured PostgreSQL database. The historical unapplied-migration blocker is resolved. **Closed 2026-09-12 by executing the lifecycle through the running application, twice.** `scripts/verify_ontology_lifecycle.py` drives the deployed HTTP API with two identities -- a steward creates a DRAFT, submits it, **tries to approve their own version and is refused with HTTP 409**, then a different steward approves it -- and reads the version back as the published head. Run 1: `version 1 status=DRAFT` -> `PENDING_APPROVAL` -> self-approval 409 -> approval 200 -> `published_version=1`, 8 of 8 checks. Run 2 proved the part a first run cannot: `base_version=1, carrying 2 concept(s) forward` -> `published_version=2` with every previously published concept intact, so the base-version and keep-every-published-key rules hold on the real routes and real PostgreSQL rather than only in `tests/test_ontology_api.py`'s in-process SQLite. Deliberately its own script and not a section of `verify_end_to_end.py`: it publishes a new `atlas_e2e` ontology version on every run, and a verifier that accumulates governed content each time is not one to run on every deploy. The first live run also caught the script using hyphenated keys, which the API refused against `^[a-z][a-z0-9_]{0,99}$` -- the route validating correctly. Do not rerun the obsolete migration/lock workaround.  |

The worst: below 1148px the closed sidebar was hidden by `transform` alone, and a transformed element is still focusable, so roughly fourteen off-screen controls stayed in the Tab order with no visible focus anywhere and Enter on any of them navigated the app. Valid markup, so axe reports nothing. Also fixed: route changes left focus on the nav button and announced nothing; no skip link; the mobile drawer managed no focus and its backdrop was a second unreachable button *outside* the drawer; two `role` attributes that contradicted their children (critical `aria-required-children`); unnamed `role="button"` SVG groups that no axe rule covers; a dialog label whose accessible name changed as you typed; and a pre-existing `RelationshipsScreen` flake that only failed under full-suite load. Coverage is now 44 navigable screens swept, 91 files / 750 tests (from 88 / 688 with one failure). Note for the register: the historical UX-5/TS-9/F21 evidence cites `ui/`, which no longer exists in this tree, so none of that remediation survived into `ui-next` and nothing was checking it. **Still human-blocked and unclaimed**: screen reader (NVDA then VoiceOver), contrast, 100%/200%/400% reflow and multi-screen acceptance. Procedure with yes/no pass conditions: `Docs/60-delivery/24-accessibility-acceptance-2026-09-12.md`. |
| R11-C3 | Correct unsafe reviewer approvals | DONE | AI safety lead | Re-measured 2026-09-12, and **the inherited figure was not a failure rate.** "7 of 12 false twins approved" counted the object types whose stored evidence clears the 0.8 threshold: both twins of every pair are scored to an identical confidence to four decimal places, because `_recommendation` is a deterministic rule over a number read off the proposal's own row and takes no proposal content at all -- so wrong-approved equalled right-approved by construction, and the number would be unchanged if every label in the corpus were flipped. Truth came from a boolean argument inside the benchmark: no author, no date, no basis, binding nothing, and in 3 of 12 cases taking the claim it judged from the pipeline's own output via an assertion, so a change to the rules engine would have redefined the case and the natural repair would have been editing the expectation. Labels now live in `tests/ar03_truth_labels.json` with the fact each rests on, how strong that basis is, a falsifier, and a provenance block stating plainly that **no human steward has signed them off** -- which remains open. The acceptance bar was stated before measuring: zero false approvals **and** at least one pair told apart, the second condition because the first is otherwise satisfiable by abstaining on everything, which is an agent that is switched off. Two adversarial pairs were added to break it and both did. **Measured: 9 of 14 false twins approved, 0 pairs told apart -- fails both conditions, so unattended review stays off and a test now pins that the flag defaults False.** A live judge given only catalog facts, and never a score or a label, contradicted 7 of the 9, so the gap is a missing check rather than an unanswerable question -- but it disagreed with itself across repeats of the identical corpus and endorsed a false twin in 3 of 4 runs, so it is not a fix, and no route in the estate is approved for adjudicating governance evidence. **2 of the 9 are outside this row's framing entirely**: bulk ownership assignment is not true or false about the data, and needs a control about authority and consequence rather than better evidence. **Decided and closed 2026-09-13 (the product owner delegated the call):** unattended review stays off permanently, and no adjudication mechanism is funded. Off is now enforced rather than defaulted: production settings refuse `reviewer_agent_enabled` (`reject_insecure_production_configuration`; removing the refusal fails `test_production_refuses_unattended_reviewer_approvals`), while development and test still accept it so the benchmark that measured it can keep running. The truth labels' missing human sign-off no longer gates anything, because nothing will be enabled on them; it matters again only if an adjudication mechanism is proposed, which would be a new row. Commit `1424dbd`. |
| R11-C4 | Prove reviewer suspension under concurrency | DONE | Runtime lead | Landed 2026-09-12 against real PostgreSQL 17.11, nothing skipped. The bound is **1 item per worker, N per fleet**, attained rather than merely satisfied at both N=4 and N=8 on one shared 48-item queue, with **zero** decisions committed past the suspension because a mid-batch refusal rolls the batch back. The earlier experiment gave each worker its own organization, so it proved tenant isolation and could prove nothing about a shared queue; a separate contention test ran several workers on one queue with no suspension at all. The intersection the row asked for had never been measured. **Measuring it found a live defect.** Guard 0's own comment said the bound holds only at READ COMMITTED and that REPEATABLE READ "runs to its limit" -- and nothing enforced it, so an operator hardening a deployment to REPEATABLE READ, the direction you reach for wanting *more* safety, silently bought an unbounded agent. With the new check disabled and the same experiment re-run at REPEATABLE READ: **47 decisions past the suspension against a bound of 4, and 48 durable approvals with the kill switch thrown.** `refuse_unsupported_isolation` now reads `SHOW transaction_isolation` and refuses anything but READ COMMITTED with reason code `reviewer_agent_unsupported_isolation` (409 on the existing API path), ordered after the suspension checks so an already-suspended agent still reports SUSPENDED at any isolation level. Proving the bound under REPEATABLE READ instead was rejected on the merits: no re-read can defeat its own snapshot, a connection per item turns one batch transaction into 1+N, and a locking re-read would make the suspending UPDATE wait behind the very batches the kill switch exists to stop. Both the measurement and the refusal are pinned in `tests/test_reviewer_agent_postgres_suspension.py`. |
| R11-C6 | Finish agent and workload identity enforcement | DONE | Security lead | Three remaining clauses closed 2026-09-12, each proven by removing its wiring, and the brief was corrected in three places. **REST was the same bypass through another transport**, and wider than the audit named: the capability envelope now gates all four product-addressed context-product reads -- the version read, its scope, the version listing and the consumer-binding listing -- which all served products under roles alone. The gate sits *above* the retirement branch, so an out-of-envelope agent cannot use the 410 disclosure to learn that a version exists; removing it fails the ordering test on 410-instead-of-404, which is the leak. AR-10's egress screening is deliberately **not** applied here, because REST serves the UI and the screening design keeps quarantined text visible to a human -- pinned by a test so nobody completes it by symmetry later. **Contract authority is no longer a bare role**, and the decisive finding was that a reviewed, eval-gated, maker-checker path already existed (`AGENT_CONTRACT_REQUEST`, already T3) while the direct PUT was its bypass, described in its own docstring as being for corrections. A direct write is now bound to the agent version's registered `owner_principal` -- the accountable human already recorded, and already the identity a contract may not name as its own principal, so no new column and no new role -- and `contract_widening` refuses **every** widening edit, PlatformAdmin included, routing it to the reviewed path; tightening stays one-principal on purpose, because an operator who must convene a committee to reduce blast radius leaves it wide. That check also closed a back door nobody had named: the PUT never writes `kill_engaged`, but narrowing `kill_scope` from ALL to AGENT **disengages the switch for every other agent it covered** -- a widening that reads as a narrowing. Releasing a kill switch now adds maker != checker against the engaging principal, read from `AuditEvent` because the model already designates that as where engage and release history lives, so no migration; engaging stays open to every contract author, since a brake only the owner can pull is not there at 3am. **Native MCP tools were not a documentation fix.** Of the seven, five are read-only value-free metadata -- but `request_data_product_access` inserts an access request, opens a governance review and commits, and `validate_sql` reaches the customer's warehouse for a dry-run estimate. Both ran with no kill switch and no contract. One gate now covers all seven before dispatch, collapsing three near-identical branches so a control added to one cannot silently miss the others, and reusing the orchestration paths' own kill reason so the two agree by construction. `tool_slugs` is deliberately not read there -- it names governed tool versions and a native tool has none, so reading it would silently redefine the field for every stored contract. **REST tool execution closed 2026-09-12, and it was two controls short rather than one.** `POST /v1/tool-versions/{id}/execute` checked the caller's role, the organization, PUBLISHED status, the version's own role binding, datasource admission and open quality incidents -- and never the caller's agent contract. An agent identity holding any of the four execution roles could therefore execute a governed tool with its kill switch engaged **and** outside the `tool_slugs` its own contract names, by addressing the version by id instead of asking through Ask; removing the gate sends a killed agent's request as far as SQL rendering, which is how the bypass was demonstrated rather than argued. The envelope half was not in the audit's wording and is the same defect: `capability_envelope.tool_slugs` *is* the per-agent allowlist over governed tools, and this door ignored it. The fix reuses `load_contract_for_principal` rather than re-deriving who is an agent, so this route inherits the boundary confusion that function already closed -- an `AGENT`-typed identity whose subject lacks the `agent:` prefix is refused here too instead of being served as an uncontracted human. Placed **above** `ensure_datasource_enabled` and the quality gate on purpose: a stopped agent probing version ids must not read datasource admission state off the difference between a 409 and a 403, and a refusal that discloses is still a disclosure. The asymmetry had already been reasoned about here in the opposite direction -- `agent_orchestrator` carries a comment demanding parity with *this* route's quality gate, because a tool version must not answer differently depending on which surface asked -- and the contract half of that argument had no such note; it does now. 7 tests, each of which fails with the one call removed, and two of them assert by *position* (a human and a compliant agent reach the next gate) because an assertion of "no exception" cannot tell allowed-through from never-reached. **The external Reviewer regime closed 2026-09-12, and it is the last of C6's named clauses.** ADR-0027's safety case for automated review rests on a tier ceiling, a sampled fraction humans read, a suspension switch, and the backlog bounds that keep the sample honest -- and all four lived in `reviewer_agent.auto_decide_tier0_tier1`, *above* the decision service, so they governed the platform's own agent and nothing else. An externally-supplied identity holding `Reviewer` reached the decision endpoints and passed only what `check_decision_permitted` asks: organization, and maker != checker. No ceiling, no sample, and **suspension did not stop it** -- the serious half, because suspension is what an operator reaches for when something is going wrong, and it was believed to have stopped automated review when it had stopped one implementation of it. The regime now runs inside `decide_review`, the single point every decision passes through, rather than on each surface that can decide -- the four holes this row found were one missed surface each, and a control placed per-surface is a control with a list of surfaces to keep current. Registered the way the target adapters are, so the policy owner depends on the service and never the reverse; the difference is the default, because an unregistered adapter means an object type nobody can decide and is noticed at once, while an unregistered guard would mean an agent nobody supervises and is noticed by nothing -- so absence refuses (INV-4), and a second registrar cannot quietly relax it. Keyed on the **authenticated principal type**, not a role or a name: `Reviewer` is a role humans hold, and a human decision never reaches the guard. **The ordering was found by measurement, not reading.** Sampling was written before the claim, which looks harmless and is not: AR-04's contention test turned `['committed']` into `['aborted:23505', 'committed', 'aborted:23505']`, because three workers racing one queue all insert a sample for the same review and the two that lose the claim die on `uq_review_audit_sample_review` -- a unique violation that poisons the transaction, so a lost claim stops being the orderly refusal the batch is built to shrug off. The guard now *returns* the sample and the service adds it only after the compare-and-set is won; the loop no longer writes its own row and recomputes the same answer from `sampled_for_audit`, which is pure and deterministic on the review id, so the two agree by construction. mypy caught a second silent no-op before it shipped: `normalize_verdict` returns `APPROVE` and the sample ledger stores `APPROVED`, so the comparison as first written would have sampled nothing at all, forever. 9 tests; three fail with the single gate call bypassed, and the fail-closed one asserts the *detail* rather than the outcome because a bypassed gate falls through to an unregistered adapter that also answers NOT_PERMITTED -- an outcome-only assertion passed for the wrong reason. **The project-level listing closed 2026-09-12, as a filter.** `GET /projects/{id}/context-products` listed a project's products under roles alone, so an agent whose envelope named one product still learned the others existed and what they were called -- the enumeration the per-product 404s exist to prevent, answered one level up. A listing has no single product to refuse, so `_envelope_listing_clause` filters instead: the agent sees what its envelope names (by key or UUID, as `context_product_violation` allows), the count is filtered with the rows so the total cannot leak how many were hidden, and an unresolvable contract or envelope lists nothing with the contract refusal audited. Tested against a real SQLite database, because a session double would return whatever rows it was handed and could not show a SQL filter excluding anything; 5 of the 7 tests fail with the filter removed, and the 2 that still pass are the ones that should. `18-agent-capability-enforcement-matrix.md` was brought back in step and had fallen a day behind: it still listed REST tool execution as role-only and called the external-reviewer gap the most consequential one left, both fixed earlier the same day. **Found while correcting it, and recorded as open:** context-product *compile* (`GET /context-product-versions/{id}/compile` and `/download`) reads a product's governed content under roles alone -- the same shape as the version reads that now gate -- so an out-of-envelope agent can still compile a product by version id. **The compile door closed the same day it was found.** Both compile routes load through `_load_source`, which now calls the version reads' own `_enforce_capability_envelope` -- imported rather than re-implemented, because a second copy is how one door drifts from the others -- above the role, purpose and quality gates, so an out-of-envelope agent learns nothing from which of those it would have failed. 5 tests drive the real routes and prove the allow path by position (a patched quality evaluation raises a sentinel, so reaching it means the envelope let the caller through); the refusal tests fail with the call removed, and the 64 existing tests that touch compile or the envelope still pass. **The native-tool allowlist closed 2026-09-13, and with it the row.** The capability envelope gained `native_tools`, because `tool_slugs` names `GovernedToolVersion` slugs and reusing it would have redefined the field for every stored contract. `native_tool_violation` runs in `_native_tool_contract_denial` after the kill switch, so a stopped agent is told it is stopped rather than that a tool is outside its envelope, and a refusal is audited as `mcp.native_tool.envelope_denied`. **Absent or empty allows none**, as every allowlist in the envelope already does -- so a contract stored before the field existed reaches no native tool until an amendment names them, and naming one is a widening (`contract_widening`), so it goes to review. That is a deliberate behaviour change for any deployment whose agents call native tools, and the fail-closed reading was chosen over silently grandfathering every native tool into every existing contract. The contract API and the Agent gateway form carry the field. `tools/list` still lists native tools by role, exactly as it lists governed tools; the call is where the envelope is enforced. 13 tests (7 parametrized over every native tool); removing the gate fails 9. The one remaining clause is a decision, not a gap: creation of a *first* contract stays unreviewed though owner-bound and audited, deliberately -- there is no prior authority to widen, and making creation review-only would break the documented correction path. |
| R11-C7 | Screen remaining MCP output and version verdicts | DONE | AI safety lead | Landed 2026-09-12. Egress screening now covers external MCP context-product fields and the prompts built from them -- screening inbound only was a half-control, since an injection returned in a tool result is the case that matters. Quarantined text is withheld and the verdict and version reported. The classifier-version policy is decided and explicit rather than absent: stored verdicts carry `screening_version` (migration `b6d24e0f81a7`), NULL reads as stale because a pre-existing row genuinely has an unknown classifier, and there is **no backfill** -- stamping today's version onto verdicts it never produced would relocate the defect rather than fix it. Staleness is surfaced, not enforced, deliberately: failing closed on a version bump would withdraw every view definition in the estate on the first deploy after one. Pinned evasion cases retained unchanged. **`tools/list` closed 2026-09-12.** The catalog was the module's last unscreened egress channel and the cheapest injection in the protocol: a tool version's `description` and each parameter's `description` are operator free text, an MCP client reads a description as guidance on *when to call the tool*, and the text reaches the consumer's context on the handshake with no tool call at all. Both are now screened live -- the same live-screening argument the context-product prose already uses (no stored verdict to consult, a low-frequency catalog read over a handful of short strings per tool, not the bulk row projection the module forbids) -- with quarantined prose withheld, the verdict reported, and one audit event per call naming every field withheld rather than one row per string. Two withholding decisions are deliberate. The **governance attestation is kept** even when the operator's prose is dropped, since it is ours and is the one part of the description a consumer can rely on; and the absence is *stated* (`(description withheld by egress screening)`) rather than left blank, because a client seeing no description should know one was suppressed instead of inferring the tool was never documented. Two things are deliberately **not** screened, both pinned by tests so nobody completes them by symmetry: `atlas__{slug}` and each parameter's *name* are structural identifiers a caller must reproduce verbatim, so screening them would withhold no instruction and break the tool instead -- a fully quarantined tool stays callable; and the native tools' descriptions are literals in this module rather than operator input, where running a classifier over our own constants would cost a call per tool per read to re-derive a commit-time fact and a false positive would silently remove a platform capability from every client. 8 tests, 4 of which fail with the screening bypassed. Still open: `tools/call` result rows, deferred on the merits, because per-row screening is exactly the bulk projection the module forbids and INV-3 remains the load-bearing control there. **Decided and closed 2026-09-13 (the product owner delegated the call):** INV-3 is accepted as the load-bearing control for `tools/call` result rows, and per-row screening is not built, because it is the bulk projection the module forbids. Every other egress channel this row names is screened. |
| R11-C8 | Make reviewer corrections traceable and reversible | DONE | Governance lead | AR-11 partly closed 2026-09-12, and the row's premise corrected: descriptions already had a full withdrawal/reinstatement lifecycle, so the real gap was narrower -- enrichment annotations and bulk stewardship only. The agent can actually approve **five** object types (the T0/T1 allowlist intersected with the evidence resolvers), not eighteen. **The finding that changed the design: compensation was not missing, it was impossible.** `apply_bulk_operation` recorded only `applied_count`, and every branch skips subjects already in the requested state -- so a `LINK_TERM` over 40 tables reporting 28 applied did not say *which* 28, and any reversal built from the requested `subject_ids` would have deleted links that predated the operation and were never its to remove. A correction doing collateral damage. Closed: `applied_subject_ids` records what actually changed (deliberately not backfilled -- on an older row an empty list means "not recorded", not "changed nothing", so those refuse rather than guess); a reversal is an ordinary `BulkStewardshipOperation` carrying `reverses_operation_id`, same table and same maker-checker, not a parallel vocabulary; `review_audit_sample_id` is the sample-to-correction edge, written in the same transaction as the DISAGREED verdict; and two bounds are now *enforced* -- the oldest unread sample must be younger than `reviewer_agent_max_sample_age_hours` (guard 8, default 168; 49 samples untouched since February had sat inside the count bound forever), and any reversal is pinned T2 read from the row, so the agent whose decision is disputed cannot decide the dispute. Only the two purely additive operation types were reversible at first; the five that overwrite values were refused **by name** rather than half-undone. Certification retraction uses WITHDRAWN, not REVOKED, because REVOKED maps to BLOCKED -- a standing refusal, stronger than never certified. Still open: downstream *harm* is unmeasured (a reversal undoes the catalog change, it does not reach an answer that cited the wrong term link while it stood); **TAG and CLASSIFY became reversible 2026-09-12** (migration `e5c8a1f4b927`): `apply_bulk_operation` now records each applied subject's before-image into `applied_before_images` -- whether a tag existed and its prior value and author, or a column's prior classification -- read *before* `apply_tag_item`/`apply_classify_item` mutate the row in place, because afterwards the prior value is gone. `RESTORE_TAG` puts back what TAG overwrote and removes a tag TAG created; `RESTORE_CLASSIFICATION` puts back the prior classification. Both restore **only a subject still holding what the original wrote**: a value a person changed since is theirs, and restoring over it would be the second wrong change this ledger exists to prevent, so it is skipped and not counted. A reversal of a TAG or CLASSIFY operation with no before-image -- every row applied before the column existed -- is refused whole (409), not restored from a guess, and no backfill is attempted for the same reason. Reversals stay T2 via `reverses_operation_id`. The migration's downgrade was exercised on the dev database both ways and the migration-ORM drift gate passes. 5 new tests plus 2 refusal cases; removing the TAG before-image capture fails the TAG restore tests. One test was wrong before it was right: it assumed an unclassified column holds NULL, but `classification` is NOT NULL with `UNCLASSIFIED` as its default -- the restore was correct and the premise was not, so the test, a service comment and the catalog row that repeated it were all corrected. Both restore events are documented in the event catalog; the *original* `catalog.asset_tag.applied.v1` and `catalog.column.classified.v1` are not, because the event-catalog gate cannot resolve this function's local `event_type` and never flagged them -- noted rather than guessed at, since their other emitters' payloads were not verified. **ASSIGN_OWNERSHIP, DEPRECATE_TERM and REASSIGN_LEAVER followed the same day**, into the same column with no further migration, so every forward operation type now has a compensating action -- and a reversal type is itself refused by name (422), because undoing an undo is a fresh, reviewed operation. `WITHDRAW_OWNERSHIP` moves an assignment the original created to a new `WITHDRAWN` ownership status -- kept as evidence of who was named, never read as the owner; nothing in `src`, `ui-next` or the migrations enumerates the statuses -- and gives a reactivated one back its status, assigner, expiry and warning stamp. `RESTORE_TERM` restores the term's lifecycle and re-approves exactly the versions the original deprecated, leaving one deprecated before it alone. It flips those rows back rather than republishing them, unlike a reinstated description, because that rule exists so an AgentRun replays the text it saw and a deprecation changed no text; a deprecated term also cannot gain a newer approved version to collide with. `RESTORE_LEAVER_OWNERSHIP` makes the leaver the owner again -- the true prior state, left deliberately to the T2 reviewer to weigh for someone who has gone -- and withdraws or restores the successor row. The still-as-left rule reaches confirmations as well as edits: an ownership row reaffirmed since is skipped, and so is a term re-deprecated by someone else or for another reason. 7 new tests; the three refusal cases move from 422 to 409 "did not record what it overwrote", with reversal types taking their place at 422. Five mutations each fail their test: removing the reaffirmation guard, the term's reviewer-and-reason guard (removed together), the successor-still-ACTIVE guard, the restored expiry, or ASSIGN_OWNERSHIP's before-image requirement. The operator runbook (`11-reviewer-agent-oversight-runbook.md`) still said TAG and CLASSIFY could not be reversed, a day stale, and is corrected with this. Operations applied before their type recorded before-images cannot be reversed. **Three more clauses closed 2026-09-13.** (1) **Maker-checker on a reversal is tested, not inherited:** `tests/test_r11c8_reversal_decisions.py` drives both doors a decision can come through -- the single-review endpoint and `decide_review`, which the batch endpoint and the reviewer agent use -- with the reversal's own requester and with a delegate acting for them, and proves another steward's approval restores what was overwritten. (2) **A filed correction is checked for a decision:** a sample resolved DISAGREED read as resolved the moment the verdict landed, so the disputed change could stand indefinitely behind a sample marked done. It now counts as unresolved while its correction waits, both oversight bounds reach it, and the age bound measures a waiting correction from when it was filed; deciding the correction either way finishes it. (3) **An agent-approved business annotation can be withdrawn.** Disputing a `METADATA_ENRICHMENT_PROPOSAL` sample files a withdrawal of the annotation version the agent approved -- through `description_withdrawal` with subject `ANNOTATION`, so the same T2 maker-checker review and handler, with the content kept for any run grounded on it -- and is refused if a person has approved a newer version since, because that version is theirs and the correction is then a new proposal. Migration `d8a3f1c6b204` gives the withdrawal row the sample-to-correction edge, which is what lets the pending-correction check reach it, and widens its subject check; it was run both ways on a scratch Postgres database with the migration-ORM drift gate green. 12 tests across the two files; removing the pending-correction clause, the pending-withdrawal clause or the approver check each fails its test. **Closed 2026-09-14: every type the agent can approve now has a correction from the sample.** Disputing an agent-approved `ASSET_DESCRIPTION_DRAFT` or `COLUMN_DESCRIPTION_DRAFT` files a withdrawal of exactly the version the draft published (walked from `published_version_id`, and refused if a person has published since). Disputing a `MODEL_IMPORT_BATCH` files a reversal batch, pinned to T2 by `reverses_batch_id`, whose changes put back what each applied change replaced -- read from the version it replaced, not from the diff-time `old_value`, which can name a description withdrawn before approval -- and withdraw a description the import added where there was none; a field edited since is skipped as stale. Migration `e6d2b9f4a1c7` records `published_version` on each applied change (not backfilled, so older imports are refused), marks the reversal and its sample, and makes `new_value` nullable for the withdraw case; run both ways on a scratch Postgres database with the migration-ORM drift gate green. 19 tests across `tests/test_r11c8_description_draft_withdrawal.py` and `tests/test_r11c8_model_import_reversal.py`; removing the newer-version check, the T2 pin, the pending-reversal clause, the no-recorded-versions refusal or the withdraw-on-apply branch, or restoring from `old_value`, each fails its test. **Downstream harm closed 2026-09-14, and the row with it.** `aida.correction_impact` finds the answers a disputed decision reached while it stood, served as `GET /v1/organizations/{organization_id}/reviewer-agent/samples/{sample_id}/downstream-impact` and as *Answers that relied on it* on a disputed sample in the reviewer-agent console. No answer text is stored, so a run is found by identity: by the exact annotation version its grounding cites (`EXACT_VERSION`), or because it retrieved or hydrated into model context an asset the decision changed (`ASSET_IN_CONTEXT`, deliberately over-inclusive), between the decision and the moment its correction took effect. Ownership changes are reported as unable to reach an answer rather than searched; one read scans at most 5,000 runs and says when it stopped. 8 tests across `tests/test_r11c8_downstream_impact.py` and the two correction files; removing the window end, the exact-version rule, source-table hydration, the column-to-table match, the ownership report or the truncation flag each fails its test. **Recorded limits, not open work:** a listing proves the change was in reach of an answer, not that it shaped it; governed tools and context products that consumed a change are not searched; a correction a steward raises directly, rather than from the sample, carries no edge to it; verdict-time distribution is reported, not enforced; and operations and imports applied before their effect was recorded stay irreversible, by design. Migration `a7c31f0b95e4`. |
| R11-C9 | Obtain release security and break-glass evidence | BLOCKED | Security lead | Target deployment, test scope and identity owners required; penetration assessment, break-glass, deployed kill/revocation behavior and policy parity remain release gates. |
| R11-C10 | Calibrate relationship confidence | BLOCKED | Metadata lead | Labelled representative corpus and acceptance target required; measure correctness independently of approval frequency. |
| R11-C11 | Certify model residency, usage and monitoring | BLOCKED | Model governance lead | Approved provider/route and residency/retention (BD-6/8); provider-reported usage, spend/latency/drift alerts and prompt/model version evidence. B2 local benchmark does not close this. |
| R11-C12 | Park customer-driven expansion | DEFERRED | Product lead | Reopen only for named customer need and scoped acceptance. Includes source-side agents/SDK, new envelope types, dimensions, historical traversal, private models/PDP, bulk feeds, Git/BI/Slack/export/PR integrations, semantic conflict UX and SOC2/ISO certification. Existing code and fail-closed defaults stay. |
| R11-C13 | Certify dynamic masking provider | BLOCKED | Security lead | QG-6 needs licensed Vault Transform or an approved compatible provider; retain current refusal and test live masking/revocation before any support claim. |
| R11-C14 | Derive connector capability flags from certification | TODO | Connector lead | Found 2026-09-19 while reconciling the 2026-09-18 functional verification (`26-functional-verification-2026-09-18.md`) into section P; the review named it and no row carried it. INV-9 says a capability the platform advertises must be one it has certified, but `ingestion.default_capabilities` returns each `ConnectorDefinition`'s hand-written `capabilities`, while certification evaluates only catalogs and schemas: explain, constraints, indexes, partitions, query history, delegated identity and approximate statistics are advertised without a certification behind them. `tests/test_inv9_capability_honesty.py::test_capability_flags_are_derived_from_certification` pins the gap as a strict xfail, so it turns red the day the derivation lands. PostgreSQL and SQL Server can be certified per capability on this machine first; Oracle, Snowflake, BigQuery and Databricks need reachable instances (R11-B5). **Remaining:** certify each advertised capability per engine, derive the flags from those results rather than from `ConnectorDefinition`, and turn the xfail into a passing test. |
