Each view reports definition availability, truncation, lineage and a digest. **Nothing outside the product leaks.** Read and write table ids are cut to the product's own `table_ids`, and nothing is counted. **Legacy artifacts are unchanged.** A definition that names no routines fingerprints exactly as before. Routine references reach every target that embeds the common context; the coverage section reaches MCP, REST and YAML only, and only when there is something to say. Authoring adds `GET /v1/projects/{id}/context-product-routine-options`, which offers exactly the set validation accepts, and a routine picker on Create draft; `routine_ids` is sent only when one is picked. Evidence: `tests/test_context_product_routines.py`; context product, compiler, MCP, Studio, OpenAPI, ORM-drift and surface suites, 344 passed; `ContextProductsScreen.test.tsx`, 10 passed. **Freshness as time landed 2026-09-16.** A product reported digests, which say whether something changed, and never a time, which says how old the answer is: the same digest reads identically whether the scan that took it ran this morning or last quarter. `context_product_coverage.load_source_freshness` now reports, per datasource behind the product's own tables, when that source last finished a discovery run and when it last finished a FULL one, with the product's table ids grouped under the source they live in so a consumer can see which part of the product a stale source is about. Both times are reported because both modes read the whole catalog but only a FULL run retires what it did not see -- an object dropped at the source stays ACTIVE here until the next full read completes. A run that never finished is not a read, and a source never read says so with two nulls, which is also the state in which the orchestrator already refuses to answer (`NO_COMPLETED_METADATA_ANALYSIS`). **It is deliberately not in the compiled artifact.** `compile_context_product` is a pure function of its arguments so one version compiles to the same bytes every time; a clock inside the hashed content would make every completed scan read as deployment drift. It travels in `generated_from.source_freshness` -- beside the artifact, not in it -- on the compile, download and drift routes, and as a `freshness` section in MCP's resource read, both rendered by the shared `freshness_section` helper so the two doors cannot disagree. Evidence: `tests/test_context_product_freshness.py` (content and hash byte-identical with and without it, on all four Atlas-native targets; the two times reported apart; an unfinished run is not a read; an incremental read never dates the last full one; a second source in the same product dated only by its own runs; another organization's tables resolving to nothing; MCP rendering the section); context product, compiler, MCP, ontology, exemplar, negative-knowledge, envelope and Studio suites, 183 passed. **Pinned meaning appears in coverage, a moved covered definition makes a product visibly stale, and pinning meaning actually works -- 2026-09-18/19.** Coverage reports the pinned ontology, glossary and semantic-model versions with their current status, identically through compile, download, drift and MCP. A product whose covered view or routine definition moved after publication reads `changed_since_published` -- so even a literal-only redefinition that leaves every digest equal reads as drift -- and the rebuild drafts a re-verification version into review. **It is never auto-published**: a test proves the published version stays PUBLISHED, the draft stays REVIEW_REQUIRED with `approved_by` None, and the view's hold stays OPEN until a reviewer approves. Adding a new version now offers the routine picker. See R11-D25: until 2026-09-19 a product that pinned a glossary term or semantic model refused every hit of that kind. **Remaining:** a stale badge in the UI -- staleness shows in compiled coverage and the review queue; a table reshape is deliberately not counted as a coverage change, or every product would go stale whenever a column is added. |
| R11-FP13 | Question-to-answer evaluation over enriched context (FP-13) | PARTIAL | AI quality lead | **Retrieval half landed 2026-09-15**, extending R11-B2's harness (`scripts/quality_benchmark.py`) rather than building a second benchmark. `footprint_enrichment_corpus.json` runs through the real `GovernedRetriever.retrieve` twice against the same deterministic catalog: once as seeded, and once after `enrich_footprint` adds a routine with reviewed lineage, a routine whose lineage is only an undecided proposal, and a published ontology concept mapped to a table. No question shares a word with its target table, so only the enrichment can reach it. Every hit is on the governed object itself (the concept or the routine) or the table its approved evidence names; that is the evidence-support check. **Gap preservation** is scored explicitly: a target whose only path is unreviewed lineage must stay unreached. Measured: recall within bound 0.0 before and 1.0 after (4 cases), gap preservation 1.0 (1 case). Both after-figures are tracked metrics in `Docs/90-reference/quality-benchmark-baseline.json`, so a regression fails the gate. **Found and fixed:** the harness created only `aida.models`' tables, so a CLI run could not have the routine and ontology tables retrieval now reads. Evidence: `tests/test_quality_benchmark_gate.py::test_footprint_enrichment_is_measured_before_and_after_with_gaps_kept`. **The evaluation the review asked for now exists as a harness, 2026-09-16 (review F06.5).** Five things were missing, not one. The answer corpus ran against a different estate than the footprint-enrichment corpus, so nothing measured answer correctness *over enriched context*; correctness was result-set equivalence against `gold_sql` with no expected answer and no expected evidence; a refusal was only an outcome bucket, never an expected verdict; and no harness captured a token or a cost at all. `scripts/answer_evaluation_benchmark.py` and `tests/fixtures/quality_benchmark_corpus/answer_evaluation_corpus.json` carry the question, the enriched-footprint objects the answer must stand on, the expected answer or result set, and -- for cases that should be refused -- the expected refusal and its reason code as a first-class verdict. Provider-reported token usage is recorded **as provider-reported**, kept distinguishable from an estimate: `cost_showback` is explicit that no dollar meter exists and that `plan_cost` is a connector-shaped proxy, so no synthesised dollar figure is presented as measured. Evidence: `tests/test_answer_evaluation_gate.py`, including a real-regression proof and full coverage of the no-network path. **Needs the domain owner:** acceptance thresholds. The mechanism ships with them unset and the harness says "no threshold set" rather than inventing a pass mark, and it is deliberately **not** in the blocking CI gate -- a green job of that name with no thresholds signed off would read as an answer-quality claim. **Not run:** the live evaluation (`--live --i-understand-this-costs-money`, roughly 5-10 model calls), which the user declined this session. It is blocked on two things, not one: those thresholds, and an enriched live datasource -- `infra/sample-source/init.sql` seeds three tables with no routines, lineage or ontology, so the harness refuses rather than score the wrong estate. **Four thresholds are now proposed, and none is set (2026-09-17).** They live under `proposed_thresholds` in `Docs/90-reference/answer-evaluation-baseline.json` with every `minimum` still null, so nothing is enforced and the harness still reports `no threshold set`; adopting one is copying a number across and filling the sign-off block, with no code change. They are reasoned rather than copied from the measurement, which that file explicitly warns against: **1.0** for refusal-reason correctness (a refusal for the wrong reason is not a correct refusal), for answer verdict (the two directions are not symmetric -- refusing something answerable is an annoyance, answering something that should have been refused is a governance breach, so the combined metric takes the stricter bar) and for unapproved-evidence avoidance (standing on unapproved evidence defeats the point of a governed product); and **0.90** for evidence support, the one genuinely probabilistic metric, matching the tolerance `quality_benchmark` already allows retrieval rather than demanding an exact figure. The three metrics only a live run can measure are left unproposed on purpose. The caveat travels with them: the case counts are one to five, so corpus size is the real limiter and should grow before any bar tightens. **The estate half of the live blocker is closed, and the row's framing of that blocker was wrong, 2026-09-17.** `infra/sample-source/init.sql` grew from 136 to 489 lines: a `warehouse` schema in `bank_demo` with 6 tables, 2 views, a materialized view, 4 routines, a trigger and a sequence-backed key, every object traced to a corpus case rather than invented. Three paths exist only through the enrichment -- a routine body is the *only* link between two fact tables (no FK, no shared stem), the abbreviated columns mean nothing without their comments, and a view joins across schemas. Corpus-named objects present in the live estate went 0 of 4 to 4 of 4, and the one `gold_sql` became executable, so `result_set_match_rate` is measurable for the first time. **The correction:** this does **not** improve the offline measurement, and the numbers are identical before and after -- both harnesses' offline modes seed `seed_catalog` in process and never open a connection to the sample source. It makes a future live run *meaningful*, not today's run *better*. The work also caught a calibration leak in itself: a table comment read "the decision each received", and "received" is a word of the approved description the corpus scores against. **The governed half is seeded too, 2026-09-18, so both halves of the estate blocker are closed.** `scripts/seed_governed_enrichment.py` publishes the `banking` ontology with `end_of_day_position` and its `closing position` alias mapped to `warehouse.fact_account_balances`, takes the settlement rollup's six discovered write edges to ACTIVE, and approves its description -- each **proposed by one identity and decided by another through the platform's own decision paths**. It writes no APPROVED or ACTIVE row itself, because an estate whose governance is fiction produces an evaluation that proves nothing; `--same-identity` shows every self-approval refused with a 409. The accrual routine's three edges are left PROPOSED on purpose, since the corpus's gap case asks whether lineage nobody approved steers an answer. A rerun is a no-op across all 193 sample-bank tables, timestamps included. The warehouse DDL was applied to the live sample source in one transaction -- an init script only runs on an empty volume -- rather than by recreating a volume other sessions use. It surfaced a seed defect on the way: discovery proposes joins resting only on matching column names, the platform correctly refuses to approve those (`RELATIONSHIP_NAME_MATCH_ONLY`), and the seed died on the first; they are now left pending for a person. **The offline harness was not offline.** It prints "no provider, no network" and reports 0 tokens, but it built its retriever from settings that read `.env`, so on a machine with an embedding provider configured it made about five `gemini-embedding-001` requests per run. The gate tests always forced the provider off; the CLI never did, so the two disagreed about what "offline" meant. `force_offline_provider` now makes the promise true on the offline branch only, and `test_the_cli_offline_mode_really_reaches_no_provider` drives `main()` with a provider configured and fails against the old harness. `scripts/quality_benchmark.py` was checked for the same defect and does not have it: it uses the embedding provider on purpose, to measure the path a deployment serves from, and its no-network promise is scoped to generation -- which is exactly why its locally higher recall is not accepted as the committed baseline. **Remaining:** the thresholds, which remain the domain owner's; and the live paid evaluation itself, which the user has not authorised. It would now measure the corpus against the estate it was written for, including a first result-set comparison. |
| R11-FP14 | Idempotent tool candidates from views and routines (FP-14) | PARTIAL | Tools lead | **Landed 2026-09-15.** A fourth task agent, `agent:tool` (`aida.tool_agent`), proposes a DRAFT governed tool from two kinds of source: an ACTIVE view whose definition passes the view-tool gate, and a routine whose stored body is proven read-only with one result query. A routine tool runs the extracted SQL, never a call to the routine. Each draft goes into the existing `GOVERNED_TOOL_VERSION` PUBLISH review, which is T2: a person decides, and maker-checker refuses the agent as its own approver. The three prerequisites are closed. (1) Draft creation moved out of the router into `aida.tool_drafts`, which neither commits nor raises HTTP errors; the router keeps both, with the same 422 and 409 answers. (2) Blocker reasons carry stable codes on `ViewNotEligibleError`, `RoutineNotEligibleError` and `ProcedureNotEligibleError` (for example PROCEDURE_WRITES, PROCEDURE_NOT_FULLY_PARSED), so the ledger never stores SQL-bearing text. (3) Idempotency needs no new table: a deterministic per-object slug turns an existing tool into a skip, and a declined object is not re-examined until it changes. An edition without `studio_semantic_and_tool_authoring` refuses the run. Also added: `tool_agent_*` settings (interval 0 means never), the registry entry, routes `/v1/organizations/{org}/tool-agent[/run]`, a Tools tab in the task-agent console, and the tool agent and draft service in the ADR-0029 import contract. Evidence: `tests/test_tool_agent.py` covers reviewed drafts, the declined writer, no duplicates on rerun, entitlement refusal and dry run; task-agent suites, 119 passed; tool and blueprint suites, 616 passed; import contracts, 12 kept; mypy clean. **Function boundary closed 2026-09-15.** A syntax guard that passes an unknown function says nothing about its effects: a SELECT can call a function that writes, sleeps or leaves the engine. `SqlGuard` now refuses, with `UNAUTHORIZED_FUNCTION`, any call the parser does not recognise as a dialect built-in and every schema- or package-qualified call, unless an operator names it in `sql_guard_allowed_functions`; the adversarial denylist still wins. The per-dialect built-in allowlist was generated by probing documented built-ins against the parser, and the repository's SQL corpora call no user-defined function. It holds on every guard: the query gateway (Ask, tools and MCP), tool drafts (a generated routine tool that calls an unreviewed function is refused), SQL validation and the evaluations. Evidence: `tests/test_sql_guard.py`; new unauthorized-function cases in every dialect's adversarial corpus; the gateway, orchestrator and tool suites. **A source that already has a tool gets no second one, 2026-09-16.** The slug check caught the agent's own repeat proposal and nothing else: a tool a person had already written over the same view, under a slug of their choosing, was invisible to it, so the agent proposed a duplicate and a reviewer had to notice. The agent now declines `source_already_has_a_tool` where any live version in that source (DRAFT, REVIEW_REQUIRED or PUBLISHED) already stands on the object: a generated tool is matched by its source binding whatever it was renamed to, and a hand-written one by the names its SQL references, in all three shapes the gateway authorises. A tool that joins the view to something else is a different tool and does not block the proposal -- the view still has none of its own. The same binding check covers routines; a hand-written tool over a routine's extracted query is not matched by name, because the routine's name never appears in what that query reads. Evidence: `tests/test_tool_agent.py` (a hand-written tool over the same view stops the proposal while the routine still gets one; a tool that joins the view to another table does not), 7 passed. **A hand-written tool over a routine's query is detected, 2026-09-18.** Matched on query *structure*, never values -- both sides parsed and rendered as the SQL guard renders a tool, every literal and declared parameter replaced by the redaction placeholder, the guard's appended row limit dropped. **Detection proposes, never binds**: it drafts a new version of the person's own tool, bound to the routine at its current definition, into the existing review queue, and the published version is untouched until someone approves -- binding automatically would let a coincidental match hold a published tool without anyone deciding. Conservative by design: different aliases, a schema prefix on one side only, reordered clauses, the query embedded in a larger one, a writing routine, or two routines with the same query are all not matched. **Remaining:** nothing ships registered (an organization must approve an AGENT-kind version and contract); a hand-written copy with different aliases or clause order is missed, deliberately; an undocumented or newer built-in is refused until named. FP18 native invocation stays deferred. |
| R11-FP15 | Change signals across definition, permission and meaning (FP-15) | PARTIAL | Connector lead | **Landed 2026-09-15.** A rescan used to leave only counters (`changed_objects`), so nothing downstream could tell which view was redefined or whose grant was revoked. `metadata_change_signal` now records one value-free row per change (ids, kinds and classes only; migration `9c3f5a1e7d42`, verified up/down/up on scratch PostgreSQL). Each row is written at the persistence point that sees the change, in the same transaction as the write. **Definition:** a view definition or routine body whose raw-text fingerprint moved is a DEFINITION_CHANGED signal, classed LITERAL_ONLY when the stored value-free text is unchanged and STRUCTURAL otherwise. A table that gains, loses or retypes a column is STRUCTURE_CHANGED; tables, views and routines leaving or returning to a snapshot are DEPRECATED or REACTIVATED. **Permission:** a grant that changed, returned or was revoked is PERMISSION_CHANGED. **Meaning:** an approved ontology version is MEANING_PUBLISHED. The design needs no idempotency key: a retried batch finds fingerprints already current, and a rolled-back one takes its signals with it. New objects and first scans record nothing. `status` (PENDING, PROCESSED) is the per-signal watermark FP16 advances. Every pull and push path passes its run id. The discovery receipt counts signals by type, Source administration shows them per run, and `GET /v1/datasources/{id}/change-signals` reads them under the catalog's own authorization. Evidence: `tests/test_change_signals.py` covers the first scan, identical rescan, literal-only versus structural, table shape, retirement, revoked grant, routine body and the read route; ingestion, discovery, envelope and ontology suites; the migration and ORM were compared on scratch PostgreSQL. **Grant classes and signature pairing landed 2026-09-15.** A grant new to a schema an earlier run read, or back after a revoke, is PERMISSION_CHANGED classed GRANT_ADDED; a changed grant is GRANT_MODIFIED and a retired one GRANT_REVOKED. The first read of a schema still records nothing. A routine retired because exactly one new signature replaced its one signature is DEPRECATED classed SIGNATURE_CHANGED, and `related_subject_id` names the routine that replaced it; an overload added beside one that stays, or two signatures swapped for two, pairs nothing. Migration `d7a4c1e9b35f` widens the class check and adds the column (verified up/down/up and against the ORM on scratch PostgreSQL), and the read route returns `related_subject_id`. Evidence: `tests/test_change_signal_classes.py`; the change-signal, processing, routine, envelope, gap and lineage suites, 91 passed. The FP16 pass already advances the watermark. **Shape change classes landed 2026-09-15.** A table's STRUCTURE_CHANGED signal is now classed by what the change can do to a query whose columns all still bind. COLUMNS_ADDED, COLUMNS_RETURNED (back with the same type and nullability) and COLUMNS_REMOVED cannot change its answer; COLUMNS_RETYPED (type or nullability) can, and outranks the others when one scan sees several. A signal recorded before carries no class and is treated as able to change an answer. Migration `b7e2d9c4f158` widens the class check, verified on scratch PostgreSQL (refused before it, accepted at head, cleared and refused again after downgrade, accepted after re-upgrade) and against the ORM. Evidence: `tests/test_change_signals.py` (added, removed, returned and retyped across four rescans). **Meaning signals exist, 2026-09-18.** An approved table, column or routine description that is superseded or withdrawn, or a semantic model or glossary-term version that is superseded, becomes a `MEANING_RETIRED` signal consumed by the existing processing pass. A superseded description counts only if its replacement's text differs, so re-approving identical text never emits. Signals are detected from the version rows every writer shares rather than written at each of the eight write sites, so a signal appears when a pass runs, not at the moment of the write. Migration `c6e2a9f41d37` widens the subject-kind vocabulary, and its downgrade refuses while a widened row exists rather than deleting it. **Remaining:** acting on permission classes, which FP16 decided against because a source grant authorizes nothing in Atlas. |
| R11-FP16 | Dependency-aware context rebuild (FP-16) | PARTIAL | Context lead | **Landed 2026-09-15.** `aida.change_signal_processing` consumes FP15's PENDING signals through machinery that already exists, not a second lifecycle (R11-S4). A view redefined in any way (a literal-only change leaves lineage intact but changes every answer) or a table retired opens or reopens one CRITICAL `SOURCE_CHANGE` incident on that table. DQ-3's coupling already fails every dependent governed tool closed on both execution paths, and a context product that denies on critical incidents stops serving. A steward resolves the hold after checking the dependents. A reshaped table, or a view whose definition the source stopped providing, opens a WARNING. A view or routine redefined structurally, or returning, becomes eligible again for the lineage agent, even if it already had edges, until a newer edge exists; literal-only changes do not. Each signal is processed in its own savepoint and ends PROCESSED with its action, which is the per-signal watermark. A second pass does nothing, and a resolved hold reopens instead of stacking. The scheduler pass `change_signal_processing_interval_minutes` (default 0, opt-in, because it opens holds) is batch-bounded per organization. Evidence: `tests/test_change_signal_processing.py` covers a literal-only view hold with the tool gate BLOCKing, a reshaped table with the gate WARNing, a routine opening nothing, idempotency, reopen, and the pass being off by default; the lineage agent's re-examination tests. **Routine tools traced and recovering 2026-09-15.** A governed tool extracted from a routine now records which one (`governed_tool_version.source_routine_id`, set by the procedure-tool route and the tool agent). Migration `e3b9f6a2d481` backfills existing versions on PostgreSQL from the generation audit record; verified up/down/up on scratch PostgreSQL with a seeded version backfilled. **Corrected 2026-09-15: bound to the definition, not to approval time.** Comparing changes with approval time let a draft generated before its routine changed be approved afterwards with no hold. Each version now also records `source_definition_fingerprint`, the fingerprint of the routine definition its SQL was copied from. Migration `f1c7a3e5b920` backfills existing versions from the routine's definition history as of their creation, verified on scratch PostgreSQL with a routine whose definition moved after the tool was generated. `aida.tool_source_binding` checks the binding at approval, where a moved, retired or missing routine is refused with `SOURCE_ROUTINE_MOVED`, and at execution on both paths through the same `check_tool_gate`, before any SQL is rendered. A routine restored to exactly the bound definition stands again; any other change needs a version generated from the current definition. A version with no recorded definition falls back to change signals after it was generated, never after it was approved. Evidence: `tests/test_tool_source_binding.py` (the binding is recorded; generate, then change, then approve is refused, and a regenerated draft is approved; redefinition, restoration and retirement at execution; the legacy fallback); tool, gate, relationship and governance suites, 268 passed. **View tools bound, dependency-aware rebuild, and the PostgreSQL journey, 2026-09-15.** A view tool is now bound like a routine tool: `governed_tool_version.source_view_table_id` with the view definition's fingerprint, set by the view-to-tool route and the tool agent. The binding module is `aida.tool_source_binding` (formerly `routine_tool_hold`). Migration `a5d8c2f4e613` backfills agent-generated view tools from their audit record, verified on scratch PostgreSQL. `aida.context_rebuild` carries a source change down the dependency chain into the review queues that already exist, and publishes nothing. It supersedes the lineage edges a redefined view no longer produces; drafts a version regenerated from the current definition for any published view or routine tool whose source moved; drafts a description for a view whose approved description was written against another definition; drafts a context product version re-pinned to a tool's newly published version; and resolves a view's SOURCE_CHANGE hold only when nothing standing on it is stale (signals processed, generated tools current, hand-written tools reading the view re-approved after the change, description current, no published product pinning a superseded tool). A refused rebuild is counted by its code and retried; nothing is drafted twice while a review waits. `run_context_rebuild_pass` is opt-in (`context_rebuild_interval_minutes`, default 0). **The journey** `tests/test_footprint_journey.py` runs end to end against a private PostgreSQL source built from the footprint sample pack: discovery through the real ingestion; the lineage agent and a reviewer; a description approved by someone else; a view tool and a context product published through maker-checker; an analyst's question ("What is the customer revenue for customer 1?") answered through Ask, which chose the governed tool and read the source through the query gateway (150); the view redefined to drop discounts; the rescan's STRUCTURAL signal and a CRITICAL hold under which Ask refuses the stale answer (422, naming the moved definition); the rebuild drafting the tool and description, then the product, each approved by a reviewer; the hold released; and Ask answering again through the rebuilt tool (160). Evidence: that journey (skipped without a reachable server); `tests/test_context_rebuild.py` (the same chain on SQLite; a hand-written tool keeps the hold until re-approved; an ineligible view blocks its rebuild; a retired table is never released; a routine tool is regenerated against its new definition; the pass is off by default); the binding, tool, change-signal, scheduler and configuration suites. **SQL Server journey, 2026-09-15.** The same journey, parametrized by engine, passes unchanged on SQL Server 2022 (16.0.4265), with no platform code changed for it. The source is a private database created inside the local sample container with the container's own administrator credentials, which the test never reads. The platform discovers it, reads its definitions, estimates plans and answers through a login made for the run that holds only SELECT on the sample schema, VIEW DEFINITION and SHOWPLAN. The view is redefined with ALTER VIEW, and the same signal, hold, Ask refusal, rebuild and 150-then-160 answers follow. **Retired and reshaped tables rebuilt through review, 2026-09-15.** `aida.context_rebuild` no longer leaves a table's hold to a person. For a table the source no longer has, it proposes a DEPRECATE review for every published tool reading it, recording the same deprecation impact a person's request records (TL-7). It also drafts a context product version without the table and without pins to tools reading it. The hold is released once no published tool reads the table and no published product includes it or pins such a tool; a rejected proposal keeps the hold and is not made again until the source changes again. For a table still in the source, reshaped or back after retirement, the hold is released once every published tool reading it still binds to its current columns (the gateway's own column check). A retyped column also needs the tool re-approved after the change, since SQL that still binds can answer differently; an added, returned or removed column does not. A hand-written or table-built tool naming a column that left is proposed for retirement, and the table's approved description must match its columns. A table description now records a digest of the columns it was written against: approval refuses one whose columns moved (`COLUMNS_MOVED`), the same rule as a view's definition, and the rebuild redrafts it into review. A view with any hold still needs its hand-written tools re-approved. Evidence: `tests/test_context_rebuild.py` (a retired table retired through review and released; a rejected retirement kept and not proposed again; a table back unchanged released; a removed column proposing retirement; retyped against added; a table description redrafted, and refused when stale); the change-signal, description, binding, gap, impact and journey suites, 73 passed. **Document claims rebuilt, 2026-09-15.** A data dictionary whose claims were proposed is mapped again once a scan in its project finishes after it was last mapped (`document_ingestion.remap_document`, using `resolve_structural_mappings`'s own rules). A row that now names exactly one live table or column gets a claim proposed for review, attributed to the uploader, unless its text was already proposed for that subject; a row whose subject left the source is UNMATCHED again. Such a document also brings its organization into the scheduled pass. Approving a claim whose table or column has been retired is refused with `CLAIM_SUBJECT_RETIRED`, so a retired object no longer receives the description; the claim stays pending and can be approved if the object returns. A row deleted outright still approves and publishes nothing, as before. Evidence: `tests/test_document_ingestion.py` (a retired column's claim refused; mapping again proposes a new match, unmaps a retired subject, proposes nothing twice, and maps a returned subject back without a new claim); `tests/test_context_rebuild.py` (after a dictionary described a column the source then added, the next scan puts the new match into review, once). **Meaning signals acted on, permission signals decided, 2026-09-15.** A PUBLISHED context product version pinning an ontology version its ontology has since published past gets a version pinned to the published one, submitted for review, the same way a superseded tool pin is re-pinned; such an organization is brought into the scheduled pass. An earlier ontology version stays APPROVED when a later one is published, so only the ontology's head says which meaning is current. Permission signals deliberately place no hold and rebuild nothing: a source grant authorizes nothing in Atlas (INV-5 / ADR-0018), no platform read consults `metadata_source_grant`, and the platform's own loss of access surfaces as a connector failure at execution. A review comparing a workspace's source binding with the source's grants would be their consumer; none exists. Evidence: `tests/test_context_rebuild.py` (a product pinning an earlier ontology version re-pinned through review, once). **Semantic model and glossary pins re-pinned, 2026-09-15.** A PUBLISHED context product version pinning a semantic model version its project has since superseded, or a glossary term version its term has since superseded, gets a version pinned to the current one, in the same draft as any tool or ontology re-pin. **Latent defect fixed:** product validation accepts only PUBLISHED semantic models and APPROVED glossary versions, so a product still pinning a superseded one made every rebuilt draft of it fail with `PRODUCT_REFERENCES_REFUSED`, which would have blocked its tool and ontology re-pins too. Such organizations are brought into the scheduled pass. Evidence: `tests/test_context_rebuild.py` (a product pinning a superseded semantic model and glossary term version re-pinned to both through one review, and nothing blocked). **A rename is followed rather than treated as a retirement, 2026-09-16.** A retired table used to leave the pass one answer: propose retiring every published tool that reads it. That is right when the table is gone and wrong when a steward approved a rename for it -- the query is still correct, the name is not. Where the held table carries CT-4's `superseded_by_table_id` and what it became is ACTIVE in the same source, the tool is now proposed *against the replacement*: a tool generated from the renamed view is regenerated from the successor's own definition, and any other tool has its SQL written against the new name by `aida.table_rename_rewrite`, which renames only references that resolve to the replaced table, keeps their aliases, never touches a name the query defines for itself (a CTE), and refuses rather than guesses when the statement will not parse or when the source has two tables of that bare name. The draft goes through `stage_tool_version_draft` like any other version -- SQL guard, placeholder check, per-object authorization -- and then into the review queue; the pass still publishes nothing. A tool that cannot be written against the replacement is proposed for retirement exactly as before, as is one whose rewrite a reviewer rejected, and neither proposal is made twice. A pointer into another source, or to a table since retired itself, is not followed. Approving the rewrite supersedes the published version, which is what releases the hold. **A data dictionary follows the same rename:** a row naming a table an approved rename retired now maps to what it became, so a document written last quarter is out of date rather than wrong; a name the source has again wins over the rename, so a restored table is read as itself. Evidence: `tests/test_table_rename_rewrite.py` (aliases kept, the most qualified name wins, a CTE never rewritten, unparseable SQL refused, dialect in and out); `tests/test_context_rebuild.py` (the tool rebuilt against the renamed table and the hold released once approved; a rejected rewrite followed by the retirement, each proposed once; a successor since retired and one in another source not followed); `tests/test_document_ingestion.py` (a row following the rename once, and a restored name read as itself); rebuild, document, rename-rewrite, worklist, tool-binding and change-signal suites, 77 passed. **Configured and observed in a deployed environment 2026-09-17.** The two passes were turned on in the local stack and a real source change was walked through them end to end: a view redefined in the sample source, rescanned through `POST /v1/datasources/{id}/analysis-runs`, recorded as one STRUCTURAL DEFINITION_CHANGED signal, processed by `scheduler:change-signal-processor` into a CRITICAL hold, and released by `scheduler:context-rebuild` -- no person in the chain. A second pass with a governed tool published over that view exercised the drafting half too: the rebuild created v2 itself (`created_by = scheduler:context-rebuild`, bound to the new fingerprint), opened its PUBLISH review, kept the hold while that review waited, and released it once a reviewer approved -- v1 SUPERSEDED, v2 PUBLISHED. A redrafted description and a re-pinned product on a deployed estate stay proven by the suites; evidence and that distinction are in [26-maintenance-loop-enablement-2026-09-17.md](26-maintenance-loop-enablement-2026-09-17.md). Remaining: a renamed *column* is not followed anywhere, because nothing records one -- a rename candidate is table-level; the lineage agent's interval stays 0 because an interval alone does not start an agent (it needs an approved version and contract per organization); the drafting half is unproven on a deployed estate until one carries published artifacts over a live view; the journey's answer comes from Ask choosing a governed tool, so Ask's model-generation path is not exercised; each engine's run skips where its local server or sample container is absent, so an environment without them does not exercise it. **Binding kept across versions, dependants warned, a dead end named 2026-09-15 (`3690475`, `fa6be9f`, `400f369`).** A held version's SQL, posted again under its tool's slug, used to become an unbound version that superseded it and ran with no hold; a new version of a source-bound tool must now itself be bound (SOURCE_BINDING_REQUIRED) and to the same object (SOURCE_BINDING_MISMATCH). A redefinition now follows recorded lineage to a bounded depth and warns the views that read the changed one -- warned, not held, since their tools still bind to what they were approved against. A description a reviewer rejected is reported as DESCRIPTION_AWAITING_AUTHOR instead of a refusal every pass repeats; the hold stays, because the published description is still written against a definition that moved. A regression test now holds the 'only affected' half: a second view's tool, description, lineage and hold are untouched by the first view's change. Evidence: `tests/test_tool_source_binding.py`, `tests/test_context_rebuild.py`; 131 passed. Remaining: both passes are wired into the fleet scheduler and ship at interval 0, so a deployment must set `change_signal_processing_interval_minutes`, `context_rebuild_interval_minutes` and the lineage agent's interval, and give the datasource a scan policy, before the loop runs unattended; the local stack still has all three at 0 (2026-09-16 review, F04). |
| R11-FP17 | Footprint operations and quotas (FP-17) | PARTIAL | Platform lead | **Queue and gap metrics landed 2026-09-15**, with FP05's register. Per readable datasource it reports pending change signals and how long the oldest has waited, the lineage agent's backlog, undecided edges, and holds. A datasource the caller may not read is omitted, never counted (`GET /v1/organizations/{id}/footprint-gaps`, Operations screen). **Bursts stay bounded by construction.** The change-signal pass processes at most `change_signal_processing_batch_size` signals per organization per pass and is off until an operator opts in. Discovery persists in `discovery_stream_batch_size` batches. Every task agent run is bound by its proposal and backlog limits. All of it runs in the scheduler or workers, never on the request path. **The register is exported as gauges, 2026-09-16.** The gap figures existed only where someone was looking at them: a screen. The failure they exist against is the quiet one -- a pass an operator never turned on, or one that has fallen behind -- where the backlog grows while every request still succeeds and nothing says so. `aida.footprint_metrics` publishes `aida_footprint_gaps` by gap kind, `aida_footprint_oldest_pending_change_signal_seconds` for the longest wait anywhere in the fleet (the longest, not an average, because an average hides the tenant that is stuck), and `aida_footprint_metrics_organizations` so a partial sweep is visible as one. **No tenant in a label:** the only label is the kind, whose values are the fixed `GAP_DEFINITIONS` set, because an organization or datasource id is the unbounded cardinality F17 records the cost of and would put a tenant identifier on a public surface; the per-organization figure goes to structlog, where retention bounds it. Every kind is published on every pass including the ones at zero, since a series that disappears when a queue drains reads exactly like a scrape that failed. The pass runs in the scheduler (`footprint_metrics_interval_seconds`, five minutes, on by default because it reads what the Operations screen already reads), and one organization's failure leaves the others' readings. Evidence: `tests/test_footprint_metrics.py` (zeros published, fleet totals summed and the longest wait taken, no gauge carrying a tenant id, a failed organization leaving a visibly partial sweep, the three ways the pass declines, and an AST check that the scheduler still calls it), 7 passed. **Landed 2026-09-16 (review F06.6).** There was no scrape configuration, ServiceMonitor or alert rule anywhere in the repository -- 19 Prometheus series across four modules that nothing collected, with OTEL defaulting to `console`. `infra/monitoring/` now carries the scrape configuration and the rules, generated by `scripts/generate_prometheus_rule.py`, over what is genuinely already measured: the footprint gap gauges, the oldest pending change signal, projection lag and backlog, retrieval duration and the HTTP error rate. Two constraints the rules respect rather than paper over: `aida_footprint_gaps` carries no tenant label by design, so an alert on it cannot be per-tenant without reversing that decision, which these rules do not; and `readiness.py` states that it "does not own the threshold at which a backlog is an incident -- it reports the two numbers; alerting decides", so the queue-age threshold lives in these rules, which is the intended split. `src/aida/cost_metrics.py` adds parser and model spend per source, keeping provider-reported usage distinguishable from an estimate and never adding a connector-shaped `plan_cost` to a token count. `src/aida/usage_quotas.py` adds real per-tenant and per-source quotas, reusing `agent_budget`'s atomic-window pattern rather than a second accounting mechanism, and restating `organization_id` in its predicate per INV-5; each new setting is registered in `scripts/configuration_decisions.py`. `scripts/scale_harness/fp17_change_burst_latency.py` drives the burst and reports percentiles. Migration `c9b3e7f15a48`. Evidence: `tests/test_cost_metrics.py`, `tests/test_usage_quotas.py`, and the footprint-metrics, configuration-inventory, fleet and tenant-isolation suites. **The burst was measured 2026-09-17, and the answer is a split rather than a yes.** 500,000 PENDING change signals over 500 tables, sustained at 5,762/s for 86.8s, consumed concurrently and out of process by the live scheduler. The catalog page holds (p50 11.3 to 12.6ms, p95 16.1 to 31.1ms, and its worst sample under load was below its worst at rest). **The footprint-gap read does not**: p95 46.8 to 148.3ms, p99 71.7 to 187.5ms, reproduced across three runs -- and since the control barely moved this is not database-wide contention but the read whose job is reporting the backlog being the one the backlog slows. Executing it also found four harness defects only execution could, the worst being a cleanup that stranded its own synthetic scope on any deployment whose change-signal pass is on. **Prometheus was verified as far as it can be**: config loaded, `promtool` clean, all 7 groups and 24 rules loaded healthy, the API target scraped, and three alerts firing correctly -- and no rule refers to a metric nothing publishes, which is now a test. **Remaining:** 12 of the 24 rules have no data, because `prometheus_client`'s registry is per-process and only `aida.main` serves `/metrics`, so the scheduler's footprint gauges are unreachable until an operator sets `AIDA_WORKER_METRICS_PORT` -- unreachable **by design, not by defect**, and deliberately not set here because opening a port is the operator's decision. The thresholds that need an operator's number are still documented placeholders, so no alert has fired on a chosen threshold. Nothing measured here touches R11-B15, which stays BLOCKED on target capacity and topology: this is one laptop, one reader, no HTTP, and it shows the shape of the degradation and no magnitude at any real scale. |
| R11-FP18 | Native routine invocation through the gateway (FP-18) | DEFERRED | Architecture + Engineering | Reopen on explicit adoption of an invocation contract after FP14: CALL/EXEC binding, OUT parameters, result sets, session and effect restrictions, and deployed-version binding. An extracted SELECT is never presented as native invocation. |

### User items 13-17 - pickup added 2026-09-17

These are authorized additions from the [items 13-17 design](../10-architecture/21-graphql-okf-and-workspace-design.md), now carried in section P. The initial pickup review found no API/format implementation; OKF export has since landed under OKF01. GraphQL remains undelivered: a design document, Tableau GraphQL parser or `GRAPHQL` channel enum is not an Atlas endpoint. Navigation/stewardship items 15/17 extend **R11-S13**, not another consolidation queue. Work in order: close UX16 correctness/acceptance; implement OKF01 and GQL01 independently; then their dependent slices. Existing FP tasks retain their own source-discovery/evidence obligations.

| ID | Work item | Status | Owner | Evidence / exit condition |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| R11-UX16 | Answer-first execution workspaces (item 16) | DONE | Frontend | **Implemented:** compact collapsible Ask history and flexible answer. **Follow-up 2026-09-17:** full-width question row, wrapping product guidance, one-column narrow form; hidden history cannot start pagination; first-page and pagination requests are cancelled and late responses discarded on source change, refresh or unmount. Paginated calls have an immediate in-flight guard; empty pages stop further loads even with a stale server total, and errors stop automatic retry loops until history is refreshed. **Wired:** Ask renders the history toggle and bounded VirtualList in `ui-next/src/screens/AskScreen.tsx`; scoped layout in `AskScreen.css`. Source-switch and hidden-pagination regression cases added to `AskScreen.test.tsx`. **Configured:** source change only; deployment/browser verification not claimed. Verified 2026-09-17: Ask plus automated accessibility sweep, 72 passed; production TypeScript/Vite build passed. **Re-review 2026-09-17:** answer precedes history in DOM and visually; desktop history is the right rail and mobile history follows the answer, with no CSS ordering override. **Views delivered 2026-09-18:** an Ask answer is Results / Query / Evidence tabs (an ARIA tablist: one tab stop, arrows wrap, Home and End), opening on Results for every run. Each view says what a reopened run still has: Results keeps the "values are not retained" note; Query reads the executed statement's stored shape -- literals already replaced -- with its tables, columns, rows and elapsed time from `GET /v1/query-executions/{execution_id}/lineage`, which now returns `normalized_sql`, `row_count` and `elapsed_ms`, read once on first open; a run that executed nothing says so. Verified: Ask tests 34 (4 new; keyboard and single-read mutants caught), the lineage shape test in `test_r11_sql01_sql_workspace.py`, full frontend suite 964 passed in 102 files and the production build; in a real browser against the running API, the keyboard model (driven by key events dispatched in the page -- the app window was hidden, so not OS-level key presses), and no horizontal overflow in any of the three views at 1440, 1280, 640 and 320 px (the 200% and 400% zoom widths of 1280). **Audit 2026-09-19 (Tool execution, Tool plans, Studio):** measured in the browser at 1280, 640 and 320 px, including a tool opened in the registry -- no page overflow on any of them; wide result tables and the section nav scroll inside their own containers. The pattern is not applied: Tool execution shows one current result with no history competing for it; the Tool plans rail is the create form, not a history, and stacks at 900 px; Studio collapses its list at 1100 px. **Deployed 2026-09-19:** images built from `d2aae14` in a clean worktree (no uncommitted file) and started with the main checkout's configuration; migrations `a5d1c8e3f7b2`, `c6e2a9f41d37` and `b3e7f1a9c562` applied; deployment parity 8 matched, 0 drifted (423 paths, 540 schemas, 276 settings). The deployed Ask Query view of a reopened Northwind run shows the executed statement with its literal replaced, its tables, columns, row count and elapsed time. **Keyboard, 2026-09-19:** with the app window visible, browser-level key input on the deployed UI: ArrowRight moves and wraps, End and Home jump, ArrowLeft wraps back, Tab leaves the tablist into the open view and Shift+Tab returns to the selected tab -- one tab stop. **Remaining:** none for this row; extending the pattern elsewhere keeps scope/provenance and adds no result-row retention. |
| R11-GQL01 | Authorized metadata/context GraphQL facade (item 13A) | PARTIAL | API + Security | **Metadata reads delivered 2026-09-18.** `POST /graphql` serves typed, read-only catalog metadata, with the library chosen by an actual spike rather than assumed. Both candidates ran against this app's real `require_roles`, async session and gate with a statement counter: **ariadne 1.1.0 raised a `RecursionError` on a fragment cycle** -- acceptance case GQL-C itself -- had no alias limit (60 aliases ran 60 queries) and no token limit; **strawberry 0.327.7** refused the alias flood and the oversized document before any SQL and batched 100 tables' columns into one query, but silently runs the first operation of an ambiguous document. **Neither met the design alone**, so the real limits are a pre-check that runs before either library sees the document, and strawberry was chosen for its per-request loaders, its code-first schema under strict mypy, and backup limiters behind that check -- with its multi-operation and traceback-logging behaviour neutralised. The dependency adds `strawberry-graphql`, `graphql-core` and `cross-web` to `uv.lock` and **moves no existing pin**; `pip-audit` is clean against both PyPI and OSV. **Parity is the proof that GraphQL is an interface and not a second catalog:** GraphQL and REST return the same objects, order, page boundaries, cursor strings and totals across five filter sets, and refuse the same five identity/scope cases with REST's own reason code. GQL-B covers direct ids, aliases re-fetching a forbidden table, foreign-key edges into forbidden tables, counts and caches across callers; GQL-C refuses 23 documents with a stable code and asserts each ran zero statements. A versioned schema artifact with its own `--check` gate lives beside the OpenAPI baseline rather than inside it. Verified on the clean redeployed image: a missing operation name and an ambiguous two-operation document both refuse `OPERATION_NAME_REQUIRED`, a mutation refuses `OPERATION_NOT_SUPPORTED`, and no principal is a 401. Evidence: `tests/test_graphql_api.py` (87); 14 injected mutations, each caught. **The parity test also found a real leak in the REST API**, recorded as R11-D19. **Per-caller rate budgets, 2026-09-19:** `POST /graphql` counts each caller's requests per minute (before the body is read) and admitted execution mutations per day, in Redis fixed windows shared with MCP (`aida.request_budget`, extracted from `aida.mcp_budget` with MCP's keys and fail-closed rule unchanged); a spent budget answers 429 `RATE_LIMITED` with `Retry-After` and runs nothing. Off by default like MCP's (`graphql_budget_enabled`, `graphql_requests_per_minute` 120, `graphql_executions_per_day` 500); keys carry a digest, never a principal id; staging and production fail closed when the store is unreachable. Verified: 6 tests (2 mutants caught) and the MCP budget suites unchanged. **Context-product reads, 2026-09-19:** the listing decision and the governed version read moved, unchanged, from `context_product_api` into `aida.context_product_reads` (REST's 315 context-product, envelope, studio and compiler tests pass unchanged), and GraphQL calls the same functions: `contextProducts(projectId, askable)` -- lifecycle view or askable, an agent's envelope filter, keyset-paged in `productKey` order -- and `contextProductVersion(id)`, which for a consumer records the consumption edge, audit and outbox event on channel `GRAPHQL`, answers a retired version `GONE` to an earlier consumer and `NOT_FOUND` to anyone else. The endpoint admits the context-product readers. Verified: `tests/test_graphql_context_products.py` 18 (REST parity for five readers in both views, cursor paging, envelope, consumption channel, lifecycle reader not a consumption, hidden-as-REST-hides, retirement), 3 of 3 mutants caught. The surface-matrix call-graph walk (`tests/support/app_surface.py`) was depth-first over paths and went exponential on these resolvers (over ten minutes); it is now breadth-first with one visited set -- the same answers, since a shortest path is a simple path: the matrix generated from HEAD is byte-identical, in 24 s instead of 116 s. **Lineage reads, 2026-09-19:** the unified-lineage graph and impact builders moved, unchanged, out of the `unified_lineage_api` router into `aida.unified_lineage_service` (the router re-exports every name; the unified-lineage, graph-store, evidence-export, MCP and candidate-review suites pass unchanged, and the one test that patched the router's globals now patches the service), and GraphQL calls them with the routes' own bounds and settings: `lineageImpact(datasourceId, nodeId, depth, nodeLimit)` with `upstream` and `downstream`, and `lineageGraph(datasourceId, nodeLimit, edgeLimit, suggestionStatus, includePendingEdges)` with `nodes` and `edges` -- each a cursor-paged connection, so the 500-object budget prices a read before it runs. These reads ask the datasource's workspace gate, as `DataSource.tables` does, and every child page decides its datasource again rather than trust its parent; the REST and MCP lineage surfaces did not ask that gate until R11-D28 closed the gap the same day. Verified: `tests/test_graphql_lineage.py` 37 (every page of both directions and of the whole graph equals the REST answer, for three reader roles and four review filters; the bounds and truncation; refusals with REST's meaning; a workspace refusal that names no table; admission pricing both directions at their page size), 10 of 10 mutants caught. Deployed 2026-09-19 at `de84027` (migrations through `0871649b2bb1`, parity 9 matched, 0 drifted) and checked live against PostgreSQL: for two focus tables, every page of both directions -- one node a page -- equals the REST impact answer, the whole graph's nodes and edges equal REST's, and a non-reader role is refused. **Remaining:** coverage reads; Agent Gateway examples and a query explorer with an introspection policy; limits moved into `Settings` and calibrated from telemetry; enabling the budgets in a deployment with Redis. The query bodies are duplicated between the REST handlers and `graphql_reads` -- no permission check lived only in a route, so there was nothing to extract -- and the parity tests are what catch drift between them. Execution through GraphQL is R11-GQL02. **Original scope, retained:** small FastAPI/auth/session library spike, pinned dependency and proposed `POST /graphql` schema; domain-service resolvers for catalog/lineage/approved context; caller/object/edge/product authorization including nested aliases and counts, request-scoped loaders, cursor limits, depth/fan-out/byte/time budgets, safe error codes and telemetry; schema compatibility gate and REST/GraphQL parity tests. Exit includes positive/negative tenant/product cases, cyclic/oversized query refusals before expensive work and Agent Gateway examples. No source SQL in a query resolver; no new top-level UI destination. Design section 13. |
| R11-GQL02 | Explicit governed execution through GraphQL (item 13B) | PARTIAL | Runtime + API | **Delivered 2026-09-18.** `POST /graphql` admits one mutation, `executeGovernedTool`, which runs a PUBLISHED tool version through `tool_api.execute_tool_version` -- the function REST and tool plans call, so role binding, the agent contract and kill switch, datasource admission, quality and source-definition holds, parameter binding, the gateway's masking, cost gate and audit are the same code, audited once. Nothing else executes: no SQL, Cypher or native call, and no `query` field reaches a source. Admission refuses a mutation unless it selects exactly one execution field, counted with fragments expanded and regardless of `@skip`/`@include` (`EXECUTION_ROOT_INVALID`, zero statements); `maxRows` is required, 1 to 1,000. A caller-scoped idempotency key claims a durable `governed_execution_request` row (migration `a5d1c8e3f7b2`, unique on organization, caller and key) before anything runs: a duplicate returns the first receipt with `replayed: true` and executes nothing, the same key with other inputs is `CONFLICT IDEMPOTENCY_KEY_REUSED`, and an outcome the platform did not learn stays `PENDING` and is never retried. The record keeps an HMAC of the request, never parameter values; rows appear only in the executing response, and the `governedExecution` query returns a receipt type that has no rows by construction (the caller's own, or any for PlatformAdmin/Auditor). Through a context product the version must be eligible and its dependencies inside the product, and the rendered statement is also held to the product at the gateway (`execute_tool_version` gained an optional product scope; REST passes none). Verified: `tests/test_r11_gql02_graphql_execution.py` (28, including masking parity with REST -- the same PII column masked identically, the raw value in neither response -- duplicate, key reuse, unknown outcome, five multi-root documents, roles, role binding, agent without contract, quality hold, cross-organization version, product eligibility/dependency/unknown, receipt ownership and no stored parameter value), 9 of 9 mutants caught; `tests/test_graphql_api.py` 91 passed after moving its mutation cases onto this contract; the live footprint journey runs the mutation on real PostgreSQL and SQL Server through the product (one execution, replay reads the receipt, the source-change hold refuses a new key) -- 2 passed, none skipped. **Settling PENDING, 2026-09-19:** the receipt names its `ToolExecution` id before the tool path runs, and once it is older than the execution deadline, reading it -- by replay or by `governedExecution` -- settles it from that row: no row is `FAILED NOT_STARTED` (the row and the gateway's `requested` record commit before any source session opens), a finished row settles as it finished, and only an unfinished row stays PENDING; nothing executes again (3 tests, 3 of 3 mutants caught). `governedExecutions(status, first, after)` lists receipts newest first -- the caller's own, every caller's for PlatformAdmin and Auditor -- settling stale PENDING ones as it reads them, so asking for PENDING lists what is genuinely unknown (6 tests; the file passes 43, including the per-caller budget cases). **Deployed 2026-09-19:** images built from `d2aae14` in a clean worktree (no uncommitted file) and started with the main checkout's configuration; migrations `a5d1c8e3f7b2`, `c6e2a9f41d37` and `b3e7f1a9c562` applied; deployment parity 8 matched, 0 drifted (423 paths, 540 schemas, 276 settings). On that stack, against Northwind's published branch tool, 16 live checks held: one execution returning rows, a replay that returned the receipt and executed nothing, key reuse refused, the receipt readable by its caller and `NOT_FOUND` to another, listed by `governedExecutions`, no parameter value in the record, exactly one `tool.execute` audit, two execution roots refused 400, a Viewer refused, and a query unable to reach the mutation. **Remaining:** SDK examples and the Agent Gateway explorer stay with GQL01's GQL-E. |
| R11-OKF01 | Deterministic authorized OKF bundle export (item 14A) | PARTIAL | Context + Semantics | **Dependency:** existing FP08 descriptions / FP12 products, extended rather than replaced. **Delivered 2026-09-17, as a pure exporter over a frozen snapshot.** The upstream specification is pinned by commit (`0b87c52c`, 2026-08-21) with its SHA-256, and **no conformance is certified**: the code records `OKF_CONFORMANCE_STATUS = "SELF_CHECKED_AGAINST_PINNED_SPEC_CLAUSES"` and a test asserts that string never reads CERTIFIED. The checker was nevertheless run over all four upstream reference bundles at the pinned revision (78 documents, all conformant), and that run found a real bug in the checker itself -- it rejected frontmatter on a reserved `log.md` by false analogy with the index rule, which upstream's own bundle carries. **Determinism is a test, not a claim:** `OkfSnapshot` is a frozen tree of JSON primitives with no ORM row, no session and no clock, the renderer imports neither `aida.db` nor `sqlalchemy`, `captured_at` is an *input*, and the ZIP uses a fixed epoch because `zipfile` otherwise stamps the current time into every local header. Two exports of one snapshot are byte-identical; a snapshot round-tripped through JSON renders the same bytes; a recapture of unchanged content reproduces every hash with the manifest differing in exactly `{captured_at}`; and a changed definition moves only its own document, so the determinism tests cannot pass against a renderer that ignores its input. No discovery timestamp enters a concept document -- `last_scan_completed_at` moves on a scan that changed nothing, so it rides in the manifest, outside the concept tree. **Identity cannot collide:** every path segment is a digest of an identity tuple rather than a row UUID (which changes on drop-and-rediscover), `signature` separates overloads and `package_name` separates a packaged member, and object kind is deliberately excluded from a table-like identity so a reclassifying rescan does not move the document. **An unauthorized dependency cannot reach a count**, because authorization is upstream of assembly: a refused datasource is dropped before the snapshot exists, so no code path could count it, nothing reports "withheld", and a link surviving to a dropped object is an error rather than a rendered dead end -- the cheap way to satisfy a text-only reading of that criterion would be to filter the index and leave the href. A verification Atlas cannot evidence is never exported: only a business-concept document can carry `verified`, only from a recorded review with both decider and instant, and the human-principal check fails closed. Evidence: `tests/test_okf_export.py` (42), 9 conformance fixtures, and an 8-mutation experiment in which every injected defect was caught by a named test. **Verified on the live deployment, 2026-09-17:** `GET /v1/context-product-versions/{version_id}/okf-bundle` against a real published product returned `valid: true` with no findings, and **two live exports produced identical bundle and snapshot digests** -- determinism holding against a running database, not only against fixtures. Paths came back as opaque identity digests, so no object name reaches the file tree. **Source-scoped bundles delivered 2026-09-19.** A `DATASOURCE`-scope bundle is one datasource's ACTIVE tables, views, routines and packages, frozen with the compiler's own coverage resolvers and rendered by the same renderer. Concepts and tool versions are excluded on purpose -- a context product selects and pins them and a source has no pin to read them at -- and the root index says so; the renderer refuses a source snapshot carrying either, or a second source. The profile stays `4`: product documents and `bundle_content_digest` are byte-identical to the previous renderer's for the OKF01 estate. Fixed on the way: `okf_snapshot._assemble` sorted objects and routines by name only, so overloads kept PostgreSQL's unordered row order and a snapshot digest could vary between reads; it now tie-breaks on the identity key (a stored product snapshot with overloads may publish one extra, log-only no-op after deploy). Evidence with R11-OKF02 below. **Remaining:** durable snapshot storage and incremental rebuild go to R11-OKF02, which needs the stored prior bundle to diff against anyway; no migration was added here, deliberately, to avoid a second head on a shared branch. Also for OKF02: a `log.md` per scope, per-tool-version concept documents, and MCP/GraphQL reads over the same snapshot (source-scoped bundles, their REST reads and a Sources entry point landed 2026-09-19; source-bundle documents are not yet shown in the Catalog object knowledge view). Import and wiki edits stay R11-OKF03 and are not enabled; one object/concept document with source/product indexes, stable IDs including routine overloads, scoped links, provenance, coverage and Atlas extension; deterministic bundle/manifest compiled from a frozen authorized snapshot of approved references. Add bundle job/download contracts without changing existing single-file compiler response shapes. Exit: PostgreSQL/SQL Server fixtures, equal-input hashes, no literal-bearing bodies/rows/secrets, denied dependencies absent from text/links/counts, safe filenames and atomic publication; label unsupported/withheld evidence accurately. Design section 14. **Export-safety correction 2026-09-17:** four regressions demonstrated raw HTML, angle autolinks and reference-style external links could pass publication. Atlas publish validation now refuses raw markup and validates reference destinations; general OKF conformance stays separate. Verified: 46 exporter tests passed (including four cases that failed before the correction), targeted Ruff and mypy passed. **Label escaping delivered 2026-09-18 (profile `4`):** every catalog label and table cell is written as literal text (`md_text`, `md_code` with a fence longer than any backtick run and `\|` in cells, control characters as spaces), and the publish policy reads an escaped character as text rather than as markup; `tests/test_okf_markdown_safety.py` renders hostile identifiers with markdown-it (6 passed, 3 mutants caught) and the OKF suites passed 120. Safe rendering at the consumer stays with OKF02. |
| R11-OKF02 | Consume and incrementally refresh the same OKF knowledge (item 14B) | PARTIAL | Context + Frontend + Evaluation | **Dependency:** OKF01 and FP16 change processing. **Storage, incremental rebuild and reads delivered 2026-09-18.** Three tables (migration `e3a9c7d51f02`): an immutable publication carrying the frozen snapshot, manifest, digests and history; one row per document with its exact bytes; and a head pointer per product version and authority. The publication, its documents and the head update are written in one savepoint, and **the head only moves if nobody else moved it first**, so a reader sees one complete bundle or the next and never a mix -- tested with a failure injected after every row is flushed, and with a concurrent publisher winning the race. **OKF-C proven live:** a seven-object product over two engines published 15 documents; one table description approved through the real maker-checker path produced a publication that changed exactly four -- that table's document, the schema index showing its description, and two logs -- while the other eleven kept their hash, and a download pinned to the first publication returned the first publication's digest. In tests an incremental rebuild is byte-identical to a full export across twelve kinds of change, and a signal that changed nothing publishes nothing. **The cache is keyed on authority, not just version:** each stored bundle is keyed on the product version plus the exact set of datasources the caller's authorization admits on that request, so a bundle built under a since-revoked cross-boundary grant is never served, and asking for it by publication id is a 404. REST and MCP read the same stored publication. The knowledge view lives inside Context Products and the Catalog evidence pane, with no new route. Evidence: `tests/test_okf_store.py`; 15 backend and 3 UI mutations, each caught. Of what the 2026-09-17 re-review asked of export, an immutable snapshot id with a download equal to the snapshot inspected is delivered by the stored publication, and concurrent publication is proven. **Correction 2026-09-18: that entry under-claimed its own change.** Early refusal and a consistent capture were built and tested in it: an oversized scope is refused from the version's pins and one column count before the freeze runs, and change marks read before and after the capture refuse a racing capture with a retryable 409 -- by mark comparison, not a REPEATABLE READ transaction. **Consumption delivered 2026-09-18: a question gets the few sections it needs, with exact receipts (design §14 steps 5-6, acceptance OKF-E).** The bundle was already many documents, and two things still kept an LLM from reading it well. A wide table was one document holding every column -- past the byte limit the export simply refused -- and nothing chose documents for a question, so an agent had to walk indexes and guess. Now an object wider than 100 columns keeps its own document (purpose, dependencies, coverage and every column *name*) and publishes its full rows as `Atlas Column Set` documents of at most 100, split by ordinal range so an appended or dropped column rewrites only the set it falls in (profile `atlas-okf-export/3`; narrower objects render as before). Concept documents now carry their approved aliases, which the freeze had been dropping -- "closing position" is how a question reaches `end_of_day_position` at all. The live check then exposed two concept defects from R11-OKF01, both fixed and each proven by a database test that fails without its fix: the freeze read `table_ids`/`routine_ids` where the ontology loader delivers `mappings`, so no stored concept had ever linked the tables it maps to; and the renderer demanded lifecycle `APPROVED`, which an ontology definition never holds (it is ACTIVE or DEPRECATED), so every approved concept exported as draft and unverified. `aida.okf_context` selects for a question: it ranks subjects from the publication's frozen snapshot (names, approved descriptions, column names and meanings, aliases, tool inputs; BM25 idf, no model, no embedding), loads only what ranked plus one hop of links (a concept to its table, a table to the routine that writes it), cuts documents to sections and long schema tables to the rows the question names, keeps meaning first within a character budget, lists what it cut, flags equal-scoring subjects as ambiguous, and answers `NO_MATCH` rather than guessing. Three doors, one store: `POST .../okf-bundle/context` (the question in the body, never the URL), the MCP tool `atlas__get_knowledge_context`, and Ask -- asked through a context product, SQL generation now receives the selected sections as grounding, every identifier must still come from the metadata context, and the run keeps publication, path, sha256 and anchor for each section. Audit records name sections, never the question. A "What an agent reads" preview in the Knowledge view shows a steward that exact selection. Proving the Ask receipts surfaced a defect that had been losing most of every run's evidence (R11-D20). Evidence: `tests/test_okf_context.py` (25), including a revoked grant removing its source from context and every context door reaching `read_published_bundle`; 13 UI tests. **Source bundles through the one store, 2026-09-19.** `read_published_source_bundle` and the product read hand only their scope rules (marks, authorization, early refusal, freeze) to a shared `_serve`/`_publish`, so lineage lookup, staleness, the read-consistent capture, the no-op rule, incremental rebuild, atomic publication and retention are one code path, not a copy. Migration `f7c2d9a4b61e` adds a source lineage to the existing publication and head tables (`datasource_id`, a `ck_*_one_scope` check -- exactly one product version or one datasource -- and source unique keys); no second store. Authorization is `READ_METADATA` on the datasource on every read, plus per schema when a workspace decides; the lineage key is the admitted schema set, and a revoked binding or policy refusal answers 403 for current and pinned reads alike. Source marks add the datasource's change signals and object rows, so a newly discovered table is noticed. Five REST routes under `/v1/datasources/{id}/okf-bundle` (inspect, download, document, publications, context), event `datasource.okf_bundle_exported.v1` with its catalog row; the Knowledge view opens from Sources (`?knowledge=1`). Verified: `tests/test_okf_source_bundles.py` 22 (OKF-D inside one source, revocation, OKF-C incremental rebuild, a racing capture refused, oversized source refused before the freeze, all five routes, the question never in the audit record, INV-6 sentinel scan, one-door structure), 12 of 12 mutants caught; UI 4 of 4; on a clean worktree of `8d931bd` plus this change the OKF suites, event-catalog, INV-4, INV-5, matrix, OpenAPI and inventory gates passed 499, `tsc` clean, KnowledgeView/Sources/Ask 67; on a private PostgreSQL database the drift gate passed and a publish and rebuild ran with both constraints enforced. The migration was applied to the dev database by an unattributed shared-tree build at 10:10 before it was committed; the schema was compared column by column with the committed file and matches. **Remaining:** the before/after answer evaluation that would *measure* whether this grounding improves answers needs paid model calls the user has not authorised; Ask now requests clarification before generation when stored knowledge equally names competing tables, with the candidates recorded in run evidence (`test_knowledge_naming_two_tables_equally_asks_which_one_before_generating`, verified in the 2026-09-18 focused suite), and the Ask screen offers each candidate as a choice that asks again naming it (`AskScreen.test.tsx`, classification in `agents.test.ts`); ranking is lexical, so a word that appears in no name, alias or approved description of the right object finds nothing -- the fix is an alias or a description, not a model; ranking candidate-count ceilings remain code constants; character budgets are now configurable through `okf_context_default_max_chars` and `okf_context_ask_max_chars`, and the Ask answer panel now renders recorded OKF document/section receipts with no-match/unavailable states (reconciled 2026-09-18 against source and Ask tests); MCP and GraphQL doors for source bundles and a GraphQL read of the stored product bundle; a live-deployment check of source bundles; the per-schema gate's cost on a large estate is not measured, and whether a scan touching `metadata_table.updated_at` forces a (no-op) re-freeze is unconfirmed. No claim of remote revocation for downloaded files. **Reconciled 2026-09-18:** the 2026-09-17 independent-recapture finding is superseded by stored immutable publications: manifest, download and document routes accept `publication_id` and pinned reads return that publication. Preserve the existing storage/concurrency tests; this is no longer a missing snapshot-identity feature. **Bounded assembly reconciled 2026-09-18:** the scope is refused from its pins and one column count before the snapshot is loaded (`okf_store._refuse_oversized_scope`, landed in `8aebc5f`), and a capture racing a source change, same-publication download bytes and concurrent publishers are already tested in `tests/test_okf_store.py`; what was still true was that the renderer built every document before checking the byte and count limits. It now refuses an over-limit plan before rendering any document and stops at the document -- rendered or carried -- that takes the bundle over its byte budget (`tests/test_okf_bounded_assembly.py`, 4 passed, 3 of 3 mutants caught; OKF suites 124 passed). |
| R11-OKF03 | Bounded OKF import and wiki-edit proposals (item 14C) | PARTIAL | Ingestion + Security + Semantics | **Dependency:** OKF01 profile/export fixtures. **Implemented 2026-09-19 (`e10bea6`), off by default.** `okf_import_enabled` ships `False`, recorded in `configuration_decisions.py`; while off both routes answer 403 `OKF_IMPORT_DISABLED` before reading the upload. `POST /v1/context-product-versions/{version_id}/okf-bundle/imports/preview` reads an edited bundle under limits and compares each document with the stored bytes of the publication it came from; `POST .../okf-bundle/imports?preview_digest=...` applies only the preview the caller accepted (`PREVIEW_STALE` otherwise). The caller's scope comes from `read_published_bundle`, the check every OKF read uses, and the bundle's manifest is checked against it, never trusted instead of it. **Where edits land, with no new table or migration:** table/view purpose and column descriptions become one pending `ModelImportBatch` per datasource under a new `OKF_IMPORT_BATCH` review, decided by the existing model-import adapter (append-only publish, `SKIPPED_STALE` at approval) and pinned at T2 so no agent can decide an import; a concept definition and added aliases become a `PENDING_APPROVAL` `OntologyVersion` on the base the bundle was exported from. The design named the document-ingestion family; that table accepts only CSV and needs one review per claim, so it would have meant a migration -- recorded in the contract doc. Imported verification or status grants nothing. **Limits** are code constants, each with a reason code and a hostile test: archive 32 MiB (also on the declared length), 22,000 members counted from the central directory before parsing, 256 KiB per document, 80 MiB uncompressed, 100:1 compression ratio on members of 64 KiB or more; frontmatter 32 KiB, depth 16, 8,000 YAML events, no anchors, aliases or tags, duplicate keys refused; 20,000 links of at most 2,048 characters; path traversal, absolute and drive-letter paths, symlinks, device files, encrypted members, ZIP64, unexported member names, duplicate stable IDs and `executor`/`computation` fields refused outright; links are never fetched and nothing executes. Round-trip contract: [`okf-import-contract.md`](../90-reference/okf-import-contract.md), which a test holds to the code. Evidence: `tests/test_okf_import.py` (15: disabled refusal; preview, apply, maker-checker refusal, approval and a re-export showing the approved text; conflict; stale preview; cross-tenant and scope-mismatch refusal; screened text never persisted) and `tests/test_okf_import_hostile.py` (89); 15 injected faults each caught. In-memory SQLite only. **Decision for the owner:** unknown frontmatter fields are accepted and listed (`UNKNOWN_FIELD_TOLERATED`) but not kept or re-emitted, because keeping them needs the parallel wiki store this row forbids; the row asked to preserve them where round-trip is supported, and the contract now declares them unsupported for round-trip -- confirm or overrule. **Remaining:** that decision; routine, package and tool-version documents (`FAMILY_NOT_SUPPORTED`); deletes, renames and alias removal; a bundle whose source publication aged out of the five kept (`BASE_PUBLICATION_NOT_RETAINED`, export again); a review-queue full preview for `OKF_IMPORT_BATCH` (the backend diff works, no UI was built); a PostgreSQL and deployed-stack run; and the acceptance that would switch it on. |
